Character
The next storyboard panels by Fred Lucky show how expressive characters
help to make the comedic intention for each setup clear. Fred reﬁned his
drawing and cartooning skills in the animation department of Disney
Studios before moving into their live-action division as a gag writer. For
several years now, Fred has been doing freelance storyboarding on four or
ﬁve ﬁlms a year, and is much in demand for his ability to interpret and write
comedy and action sequences.

A storyboard by Fred Lucky.
A storyboard by Fred Lucky.
Interpretation
Above all, the storyboard conveys the shot ﬂow of the scene, which is the
combination of dramatic and graphic design. In the case of the best
production illustrators, it also conveys mood and tone, but practically

speaking, the use of viewpoint, lens perspective, and narrative motion are
paramount. An excellent example of all of these qualities is seen in Harold
Michelson’s storyboards for The Graduate, beginning on page 64. The
production design is by Richard Sylbert.

Harold Michelson’s storyboards for The Graduate, Production design by
Richard Sylbert.

Harold Michelson’s storyboards for The Graduate, Production design by
Richard Sylbert.

Harold Michelson’s storyboards for The Graduate, Production design by
Richard Sylbert.

Harold Michelson’s storyboards for The Graduate, Production design by
Richard Sylbert.

Harold Michelson’s storyboards for The Graduate, Production design by
Richard Sylbert.
Even without a knowledge of the story, the action of the boards is easy to
read and the cutting rhythm is clearly established. The individual and
continuity design of panels 7, 8, and 9 is particularly elegant, with several
effects cooperating to determine our relationship to the characters and to
express Ben’s point of view and daydreaming. The angular use of the frame
is subtle and fully motivated by the staging, as are the dramatic backlighting
and shadow effect on the water. This is visual storytelling of a very high
order. Graphic and compositional statements are never used as an end in
themselves, but are in complete harmony with the subject and narrative
intention. It’s about the story, not the storyboard art.
Adaptation
In a sense, all storyboards are adaptations, since they are transposed from a
screenplay. Unlike a play or a novel, however, a screenplay is conceived as
an intermediary form, a blueprint for the actual medium in which the
narrative will appear. A screenwriter strives to be visual, to write drama that
can be seen and heard; in fact, it needs to be seen and heard to be fully
understood. In theory, the storyboard artist merely pictorializes the ideas in
the screenplay, but in practice, the storyboard may come very close to being
another draft of the screenplay if only to polish some of the ideas. In the
same way that screenwriters describe visual elements, some storyboard
artists suggest literary ideas, restructure scenes, add story elements, and
contribute dialogue.
To get a better sense of how a story can be shaped for the screen, we can
look at the way ﬁlmmaker Steven Spielberg adapted two scenes from J.G.
Ballard’s autobiographical novel Empire of the Sun. In this case, Spielberg
is largely responsible for the conceptual content of the storyboard.
Fortunately, we have several stages of the process to compare: The novel;
two drafts of the screenplay, the ﬁrst draft dated January 7, 1986, by
playwright Tom Stoppard and the revised third draft dated September 12,
1986, by Menno Meyjes; and, of course, the storyboard. The storyboard by
David Jonas begins on page 71.

The Novel
The story follows the exploits of an eleven-year-old British boy, Jim, living
in Shanghai with his wealthy parents. The year is 1941, at the moment the
Japanese invade Shanghai. Our ﬁrst scene opens at dawn in Jim’s room at
the Palace Hotel. In the novel, he is already awake and dressed in school
clothes. He goes to the window overlooking the Shanghai waterfront along
the Yangtze River. Jim watches two picket boats ﬁlled with Japanese
marines and a motor launch of ofﬁcers leave a Japanese gunboat. They
board an American ship and a British ship. The launch ﬂashes a message by
signal lamp to the gunboat. Jim tries to signal back, moving his arms in
semaphores that he never quite learned in the scouts. Within seconds, the
gunboat ﬁres a shell at the British ship and the shockwave rocks the hotel.
Startled Jim jumps back to his bed. This is the beginning of the invasion of
Shanghai and within minutes, panicked guests are ﬂeeing the hotel. Jim’s
father comes into his room and tells Jim that they are leaving in three
minutes. Sitting on the bed, Jim considers that he is responsible for the
Japanese attack.
He realized that he himself had probably started the war, with his confused
semaphores from the window that the Japanese ofﬁcers in the motor launch
had misinterpreted.
The Screenplay
Here is Tom Stoppard’s adaptation of the same scene from the novel. The
scene opens in Jim’s hotel room. This time Jim is asleep. He is awakened at
dawn by the sound of a close ﬂying plane. He gets out of bed and goes to
the window.
The prolonged action of the Japanese marines boarding ships that appears in
the novel is condensed in the screenplay to Jim’s watching the Japanese
gunboat moving in the river. He backs away from the window and turns on
the bedside light. An exterior shot of the hotel lets us see the light in the
window of the generally dark hotel.
the last few pages the pan We cut to a short time later and Jim is dressed in
school clothes. He picks up a Latin Primer, which is near a small toy plane
on a table alongside the bed, but is soon attracted to the action outside the

window. The Japanese gunboat in the middle of the river is sending a
message to another boat with a signal light. Jim returns to his bedside and
picks up a ﬂashlight. Cut to a new shot outside the hotel. This is a wide shot
of the hotel, the river, and the gunboats. In the dim predawn light, Jim’s
ﬂashlight blinks in the window of his hotel room. After a moment of
signaling, the gunboat cannons roar and Jim’s room is lit up with a brilliant
ﬂash of light. Jim falls backward. Within seconds, Jim’s father rushes in
calling his name.
JIM
I didn’t mean it! It was a joke!
As it turns out the ﬁrst and revised third drafts of the screenplay are
essentially identical. We can compare this with the storyboard, which is a
fairly straightforward staging of the screenplay. The major changes, the
ones that make the scene truly cinematic, are added in the ﬁlm. Beginning
below is a condensed version of the storyboard.

David Jonas’s storyboard for Empire of the Sun.

David Jonas’s storyboard for Empire of the Sun.

David Jonas’s storyboard for Empire of the Sun.

David Jonas’s storyboard for Empire of the Sun.

David Jonas’s storyboard for Empire of the Sun.

David Jonas’s storyboard for Empire of the Sun.

David Jonas’s storyboard for Empire of the Sun.

David Jonas’s storyboard for Empire of the Sun.
Now that we have looked at all the sources for the scene — the novel,
screenplay, and storyboard — we can consider how the scene was actually
shot.
The Sequence as Filmed
The scene opens with various shots of the Shanghai waterfront at dawn. Cut
to the shadow of a plane on the ceiling of Jim’s darkened hotel room. The
camera pans with the shadow made by a toy plane Jim holds in the beam of
his ﬂashlight. Continue the pan down the wall and over to Jim, who is
dressed and lying in bed. The mournful sound of a ship’s horn draws Jim’s
attention to the window overlooking the river.

New angle at the window with Jim. He watches the Japanese gunboat
signaling the shore with a light.
Cut to three exterior medium shots of the Japanese crewman sending
signals.
Cut to a wide shot of the river, the hotel, and the gunboat in the background.
In this shot, we see the two Japanese ships signaling back and forth.
Suddenly a third light begins signaling in one of the upper ﬂoor hotel
windows.
Cut to Jim’s hotel room with him signaling with his ﬂashlight.
We can pause here to review how the cinematic structure evolves. First,
there is the use of the spotlight and the toy plane. Spielberg has condensed
two story elements into a single image that graphically moves us to Jim. It’s
a choice evocation of childhood and it introduces the ﬂashlight and the toy
plane, which will become important in later scenes.
Another visual decision substitutes the ﬂashlight for the semaphore signals
described in the novel for the obvious reason that the light can be seen from
a distance. This opens up new possibilities for shots. It is also far more
plausible that Jim would imagine that he had inadvertently sent some
provocative signal to the Japanese gunboat with the ﬂashlight than with
semaphores.
In the ﬁlmed sequence, unlike the screenplay and the storyboard, we never
see Jim return from the window to pick up the ﬂashlight. By introducing the
ﬂashlight in the opening shot with the toy plane, it is unnecessary to show
Jim taking it from the nightstand. By omitting the in-between shots of Jim
picking the ﬂashlight up and going to the window, the scene is streamlined
and the audience is surprised (but not confused) when Jim’s signal light
appears above the Japanese signal in the wide shot of the river.
Spielberg adds a ﬁnal variation on the storyboard in the ﬁlmed staging.
When Jim is startled by the gun blast and throws himself backwards onto
the ﬂoor, the camera pans with him but leaves him in midair, panning
quickly to record the moment he hits the ground in the reﬂection of three
standing mirrors that are connected by hinges. One of the panels shaken by
the blast swings inward and frames the doorway in time for us to see his

father enter the room. Jim is reﬂected in two of the mirrors and his father is
reﬂected in a third. In this way, three shots are combined into one. This is so
adroitly executed that the effect is all but invisible.
Empire used the talents of two production illustrators, David Jonas and art
director Ed Verreaux, who worked on the project for nearly a year. Using
photographs shot in Shanghai, the illustrators worked at the art department
in Spielberg’s Amblin Entertainment complex in Universal City. Like many
of the production illustrators I spoke with, Jonas began working in
production illustration in the Disney animation department, honing his
drafting and storytelling skills before moving to live action.
The Empire of the Sun boards, originally drawn to ﬁll an 8 1/2 × 11 inch
page, are exceptionally detailed and show Jonas’s wonderful compositional
sense and ﬁgure drawing. This level of execution is as complex as
storyboard illustration ever becomes, particularly since nearly every scene
of the movie was illustrated with the same craftsmanship. For today’s
complex action pictures, there are as many as 5 or 6 storyboard artists and
several more concept artists.
Seeking a Career in Production Illustration
Storyboarding is a highly specialized skill offering little of the personal
recognition artists in other ﬁelds can expect. To a great extent, production
illustrators are hired to work out someone else’s ideas and are then under
great pressure to complete the work in a short time. For some, the
collaboration with a good production designer and director and the
opportunity to see their sketches turned into a great movie is sufﬁcient
reward. In today’s visual marketplace and an era when online portfolios can
reach thousands of potential assignments, a production illustrator can work
in game design, TV, and motion pictures, web toons, book illustration, and
graphic novels to stay busy. It’s much easier today to mix independent work
and fulltime employment at a studio than ever before.
Instruction
There are many, many sources of information online for traditional and
digital art training. YouTube has thousands of free tutorials by artists for
artists many of which will lead to a site with subscription-based courses.

There are many online art schools both with and without credit or certiﬁcate
programs. Lynda.com, Pluralsight, Video Copilot, Udemy, and many, many
other sites offer courses in digital software as well as speciﬁc art or visualeffects skills such as ﬁgure drawing or perspective. Many traditional art
schools now offer degree programs online. Storyboard courses are
available, but with fewer options than the broader category of digital
illustration. Even if you are interested in production illustration for live
action, you will probably have better luck ﬁnding storyboard instruction at
an animation school. Storyboarding is essential in animation; in live action
it’s not.
The good news is that traditional training at a ﬁne arts or commercial art
school is still a very good foundation for production designers, art directors,
and production illustrators. Many art schools offer courses in advertising
art, which include television commercial storyboarding in the curriculum.
However, most art schools tend to focus on foundational drawing and
painting. Though drawing skill is important, a production illustrator is
primarily a ﬁlm designer whose concern is really cinematography and
editing. Courses in ﬁlm history, technique, and basic photography are,
therefore, a necessity no matter how strong your illustration skills are.
Unions
There used to be two California organizations to speciﬁcally represent
storyboard illustrators. The ﬁrst was the Illustrators and Matte Artists Local
790 IATSE (International Alliance of Theatrical Stage Employees), whose
jurisdiction included live-action storyboard art and production illustration.
The second union was the Motion Picture Screen Cartoonists Local 839,
also afﬁliated with the IATSE, but this union only represented animators. In
2008, the two unions were merged into Local 800 of the Art Directors Guild
that only a few years earlier had merged with the Scenic, Title and Graphic
Artists union to form the Art Directors Guild & Scenic, Title and Graphic
Artists.
Today, the craft unions in the motion-picture industry exert far less control
than in the past, particularly since there are so many independently
produced nonunion pictures made outside of Los Angeles. While the cost of
making a picture goes up much faster than the rate of inﬂation, the main

reason why even small motion pictures cost millions of dollars is due to the
extraordinary salaries of producers, directors, and stars, along with the
expense of marketing a picture. Comparatively speaking, the cost of skilled
union personnel is a bargain.
Further information on the Art Directors Guild can be reached here:
The Art Directors Guild
11969 Ventura Boulevard, 2nd Floor
Studio City, CA 91604
T (818) 762-9995
https://www.adg.org

4 Visualization: Tools and Techniques
With only a few exceptions, ﬁlmmaking tools today are digital. In previs,
the most important exceptions are pencil and paper. Sketching by hand is
still remarkably efﬁcient at a time when the Wacom tablet and your image
editing software of choice are standard issue for animators, visual-effects
artists, and production artists.
Drawing
There are many digital sketch tools out there and more coming all the time.
The iPad and its competitors are upping the features as prices fall. There are
drawing slates and there are slate/monitors. Slates are basically pen inputs
for computers. You draw on a ﬂat drawing surface while watching the
results on your computer screen. Slate/monitors like the Wacom Cintiq let
you draw directly on a computer screen optimized for drawing. Slates
(without the monitor) are down to less than $100 and this is a simple stylus
and a drawing surface in the 6–8 inch size range with larger surfaces
coming in around $300–$400. Slate/monitors are more expensive, but quite
frankly, they are the future and great for tracing or retouching photos and
can be found in the $500–$1,000 range. In Hollywood, most of the
storyboard and sketch artists have moved to digital drawing. Fortunately, in
the past few years, there has been an explosion of new drawing hardware
and apps offering new features at low prices.

Wacom Tablet
Image Courtesy of: Wacom Co., Ltd.
Drawing and Painting Software
Software options for digital drawing and painting begin with the usual
suspects, Adobe Photoshop and Corel Painter, but several newer programs
are offering features that give the status quo a run for its money. Smith
Micro’s Moho is drawing/animation software while Clip Studio PAINT EX
is a general drawing and painting tool optimized for comic book art. Both
are tops in their class. We’ll get back to Smith Micro in the 3D software
section. There’s also Autodesk Sketchbook Pro, Rebelle 2 (digital natural
media painting), ArtRage, Black Ink (Windows only), PaintTool SAI
(Windows only), Toon Boom (also widely used in Hollywood animation
studios), and Mischief.
Asset-Driven Design

There are billions of digital pictures, models, design templates, and video
and audio samples available online. Much of it is free. There’s no reason to
create everything from scratch because many of the pictures/sound/audio
you use will not be seen in the ﬁnal movie. This is good, because pre-made
assets can be used by anybody and you don’t want some design element of
your movie showing up on YouTube before your movie opens. Fortunately,
you don’t see the blueprint for a building; you see the building. Art for preproduction is used until the movie is made; after that, it’s disposable.
You can also create your own library of pre-made assets. Why not keep a
folder of wide/medium/close-up shots on your computer with screen
captures or shots downloaded from your favorite movies? Create categories
for close-ups, shots, reverse shot patterns, medium shots, two-shots, singles,
etc. Sergio Leone close-ups will not be the same as Christopher Nolan
close-ups. You can trace them or use them directly in storyboards to show
the production designer or art director how you envision the scene. This is
all asset-driven design, a growing trend in the visual arts. Numerous
commercial sites sell pre-created art templates for use in After Effects,
Photoshop, and many other programs including 3D animation applications.
The quantity of assets is growing exponentially, because there are many art
marketplaces online offering the design aids of a huge number of digital
artists. Every illustrator or photographer or 3D modeler is able to upload
work to sell online in a wide range of styles. Marketplaces often offer
tutorials free and paid for techniques related to their assets.
Premade assets are hugely helpful for art reference, but there are other
solutions as well to help with your drawing. For example, most artists keep
a camera or a small hand mirror handy so they can pose for their own art.
An iPhone with a tripod works ﬁne and is easily transferred to your hard
drive. If you draw on your iPad and also use the built-in camera, you skip
the need to import. That’s a cool workﬂow.
When you start to storyboard your movie, write a shot list or indicate
directly in the script where close-ups and medium shots will be used. The
idea is to have reference for thumbnail drawings, or photoboards.
While digital photos eliminate the need to draw, you now need actors,
friends, or interested bystanders to be in your photos. One thing worth

keeping in mind about digital photography and videography: It’s very easy
to shoot tons of material since digital media is free. But your time is
anything but free when have you go through hundreds of photos and hours
of video. If you have the time, that’s ﬁne, but trying to ﬁnd the right shot
and sequence of shots requires some discipline if you are working within a
budget. The solution is to be organized. Get in the habit of using sensible
naming conventions and a folder hierarchy that is as simple as possible.
Naming conventions: Be consistent. This will be a huge help when you’re
performing searches on your computer. A month after you’ve storyboarded
a scene consisting of many folders, chances are you will have forgotten
where things are unless you ﬁle intelligently. Here’s the basic workﬂow.
• Add pictures and notes in the margins of the screenplay
• Create a shot list
• Make list of assets needed (cars, houses, locations, people, etc.)
• Find the needed assets online
• Organize the assets in folders
• Use the assets to make your storyboard or your photoboard.
Presentation
So now you have 100 storyboard panels with drawn art, some photos, and
images downloaded from the Internet. Fine. What’s the best way to show it
to people? There are many options. For one, Layers in Photoshop. You click
the layers on and off in the stack to reveal the storyboard panels
sequentially or you can do a similar process using a .pdf ﬁle or PowerPoint.
You can narrate and advance through the images at your own pace. This is
basically a slideshow. The next step up in sophistication is a slideshow plus
motion, transitions, music, and sound. Avid, Final Cut Pro, Premiere, After
Effects, iMovie, and similar editing software have more than enough
features to make a professional presentation.
Sometimes you will need to print out your boards. You can set this up
yourself in Photoshop or download any of dozens of free templates
available online. Software like Toon Boom and Moho have templates built
in. Nowadays, all work is shared on the Web so most people are
accustomed to seeing art on a LED screen. For color art, a digital monitor is
far more vibrant than an ink-jet print viewed under typical interior lighting.

LED panels are only getting lighter and brighter and this is the optimal way
to show your artwork at its best. iPad and its competitors are also a great
way to show a production art with music and sound effects.
Dedicated Storyboard Programs
Several of the drawing programs already mentioned have tools for
animation design, but are also general use drawing and painting programs,
everything needed to create 2D animation. There are a few programs,
however, explicitly aimed at ﬁlmmakers interested in creating their own
storyboards and animatics for live action. There are both 2D and 3D
offerings. PowerProduction Software has been around the longest and has
several storyboarding products including their ﬂagship product StoryBoard
Artist Studio Edition. This is a very capable software program that is a
drawing environment but also sports a powerful asset-based approach. That
means the program comes with hundreds of 2D characters, sets, and props
that can be rotated and easily scaled and positioned in the frame. There are
also dozens of pre-staged arrangements of characters that ship with the
product. These are layered onto a canvas over any of hundreds of common
interior and exterior locations. It took quite a few years for this program to
have sufﬁcient assets to make this work, but recently the database has
grown impressively. What you won’t get from the art is the cohesive art
direction or speciﬁc lighting you would get from an artist, but for
previewing scenes with everything at your ﬁngertips, Storyboard Artist
Studio has little competition. Lastly, and very importantly, the program
provides a timeline editor with effects, transitions, and full sound support.
Overall, a very capable all-in-one 2D previs environment.

Storyboard Artist Software is a 2D digital previs program that includes a
timeline editor.
3D Previsualization
This is the major leagues for ﬁlm design. At the high end, you have
dedicated previs service providers in Hollywood, and many VFX studios
around the world now offer this service as well. But staying in the DIY
arena, the top software for 3D is the same list we’ve had for a couple of
decades: Autodesk Maya and 3D Studio, Cinema 4D, Houdini, open-source
Blender, and LightWave supplemented by specialty tools such as Z-Brush.
The animation created in 3D then moves into a compositing program such
as Nuke or After Effects and then into an editing program such as Avid,
Final Cut Pro, Premiere, or even iMovie. These are all relatively complex
tools to learn with too much technical overhead for most ﬁlmmakers who
want to visualize their movies, but do not expect a career in visual effects
for animation. Having said that, there are many 3D-savvy ﬁlmmakers out
there for whom 3D is their sketchbook. Simpliﬁed 3D tools for directors
with little training in 3D software include iClone and FrameForge, and less
ﬁlm-speciﬁc software Poser and DAZ Studio, both essentially 3D
illustration tools.

Poser, the program created by Larry Weinstein, was supposed to be a digital
mannequin for artists back in 1995 and ended up becoming the prototype
for every previs product that followed. Poser evolved into a favored tool
because it pioneered asset-driven software. This meant that Poser
mannequins could be customized and modiﬁed by Poser users. It wasn’t
long before a thriving marketplace for Poser artists to buy and sell original
and modiﬁed models appeared. Trust the power of the crowd to generate
tens of thousands of models, props, costumes, and locations in the space of
15 years. Poser is not optimized for previs, and is really designed for
illustrators, but it remains a great asset for the previs process. Typically, CG
savvy ﬁlmmakers will end up using several different programs and
techniques in combination to design their movie.
Like Storyboard Artist Studio Edition, FrameForge has over several years
of development and versions put together a very powerful previs tool.
FrameForge was created from the beginning for ﬁlmmakers, and the tools
are optimized for this purpose. There is a large asset base of characters,
poses, props, and locations that ship with the product and other themed sets
of characters and props. Scenes are in 3D and there is a full complement of
the most commonly used professional virtual cameras and lenses, dollies,
boom arms, cranes, and lighting gear. FrameForge allows you to create fully
dimensional, animated scenes for previs and even includes the ability to
reverse engineer the spatial information from a location photograph used
for a background. This means your staging for a particular lens will be
accurate for that particular photograph.
iClone is another powerful environment based on a game engine for
realtime performance. This is an industry trend, but iClone is one of the
most highly evolved real-time animation programs, with a character creator
module and a growing database of assets. The look of iClone is generally
more sophisticated than FrameForge because while it’s easily adapted to
previs, it’s also capable of producing game-level cinematics.

FrameForge allows you to use actual cameras. In this scene, its the Arriﬂex
D-21..
FrameForge has the ability to print out pages in many conﬁgurations. This
one includes the lens information, a schematic of the set, and all the
elements on the set, including the camera and dolly track.
DIY Previs
The above tools are empowering and exciting, but despite all the
optimization and premade assets available, creating a 3D animated previs
sequence or a PowerPoint slideshow of storyboards with music is a
signiﬁcant undertaking. Sometimes beat boards are as much as you’ll need,

depending on the way you direct. The more complex a shoot and rigid the
schedule, the greater the need for a detailed previs. Of course, you don’t
have to do everything yourself. Finding a good CG generalist to set up all
your shots in FrameForge is a smart choice if it’s in the budget. You can
then go in and quickly re-stage the scene by learning a few simple
navigation commands. That way your time will be used making creative
decisions and evaluating the previs not spending time on the busy work of
setting up scenes.
The following previs for an elaborate action set piece from Deadpool,
directed by Tim Miller, shows state-of-the-art previs with ﬂuid character
movement based on motion capture as well as key frame and physics
simulations. When necessary, previs of this kind is based on real-world
dimensions of objects and locations, and uses technically accurate cameras
and lenses. This means that camera placement and logistical decisions
based on the previs will be the same as if they were on location or on set.
Previs at this level is costly and mainly used for action, fantasy, and sci-ﬁ
blockbusters. In fact, they are essentially low-budget animated movies
except that they will never be released.
In the Deadpool previs, three panels appear within each frame. At the top is
the animated previs. Lower left is the motion capture session to record the
motion of the actors that will be imposed on the 3D characters in the previs.
The live-action footage based on the sequence runs in the lower right panel.
Compositing
When you generate digital artwork, video, audio, and 3D animation for an
animatic or previs you will combine these in an editing or compositing
program to create the ﬁnal presentation. Most nonlinear editing software
have some compositing tools built in, but there are dedicated programs that
are more powerful. The hands down winner is this category is Adobe After
Effects, which is to compositing what Photoshop is to image editing. It’s the
gold standard. There are worthy competitors particularly on the high end for
motion pictures, but for all-around usability, simplicity of use, and features,
it’s an extremely powerful environment for combining footage and creating
VFX animation from scratch. Other excellent compositing software
includes Black Magic Designs, Fusion, and Nuke from the Foundry. These

last three products are typically used in a studio pipeline setting in visual
effects studios although this is not a requirement.
Digital Video
It’s now so easy to shoot HD video and import it into an editing program
that sometimes it seems it would be easier to shoot video to record a scene
for previs instead of going to the trouble of replicating reality with
storyboards and 3D animation. This is true, but we’re back to the actor,
location, and logistics problem. Sure, you can shoot and edit on your
iPhone, but even shooting run-and-gun style can be a lot more complicated
than it sounds. Still, you can certainly previs many parts of most movies
fairly easily with a smartphone. Shooting an entire movie to previs the
actual movie is not practical, but it can be ﬁne for selected dialogue scenes.
A ﬁnal animatic can be a mix of video, storyboards, and 3D animation.
Whatever conveys the basic feel of the movie and important story points is
a good solution.

Blur Studios previs for Deadpool. ©2016 Twentieth Century Fox. All rights
reserved.

Blur Studios previs for Deadpool. ©2016 Twentieth Century Fox. All rights
reserved.

Blur Studios previs for Deadpool. ©2016 Twentieth Century Fox. All rights
reserved.

Blur Studios previs for Deadpool. ©2016 Twentieth Century Fox. All rights
reserved.
Rip-O-Matics

YouTube and the current fashion of sampling art is ﬂying high in
Hollywood with rip-o-matics. These are typically trailers or teasers to sell a
project to the studios, a distributor, or independent investors. Rip-o-matics
are popular because every explosion, location, car chase, and dialogue
sequence close to what your movie requires is probably online in an
existing movie. This is remarkably true if not a bit disturbing. So it’s
possible to create a powerful, fully executed previs sequence or a trailer
with footage liberally lifted from existing movies. A good editor can
assemble a scene this way so a viewer would not easily know the source.
Rip-o-matics are common sales tools nowadays, but the point here is that if
you need shots of a Civil War battle and can’t draw it or create it in 3D,
chances are there’s a shot close to what you need in some movie in the past
50 years, and it’s on YouTube. Be careful to observe copyright laws for any
material used.
Sound and Scores
Many ﬁlmmakers like to cut to music. Sound effects can be just as powerful
— for example, watching a slideshow of beat boards for a scene in a city, a
background sound effect of trafﬁc makes the ﬁctional world come alive. All
this can be had for a price online and there are quite a few sites with free
sound effects and musical selections. Most sound effects sites are indexed
by subject with thousands of sounds in multiple formats for download.
Most sounds are in the $2–$10 range and are easily previewed on the host
site.
Analog
Digital is everywhere, but some analog tools and techniques are still
available, and that’s a good thing. Not every director needs to know the
Adobe Creative Suite to make a movie, and there are many times when
shooting a physical object in the real world or drawing them using real
pencil and paper are faster and more fun than their virtual doppelgangers.
Recently, a new class of anatomically accurate posable ﬁgures have arrived.
Stickybones, Bandai S.H. Figuarts Body-kun, Dolk SFBT, and Figma make
a variety of plastic ﬁgures to help artists draw the human ﬁgure from any
angle. They’re pricey, but enormously helpful for any storyboard artists.

There are also much less expensive toy soldiers that can be placed in
miniature sets.
S.H. Figuarts makes posable human ﬁgures for artists.

Steven Spielberg using toy soldiers for previsualizing Raiders of the Lost
Ark.
What’s Next
The software and hardware mentioned above is always evolving and new
versions of the software come out far more often than revisions to Shot by
Shot, so there is now a Shot by Shot website, www.shotbyshotbook.com,
where it’s much easier to update information on digital tools and resources.
The Web address is in the back of the book.
As for the future, previs and asset-based design will only grow as more and
more individual artists offer premade customizable design templates for just
about everything that ﬁts in a frame or can be loaded up on YouTube. This
is art in the age of digital reproduction.
The Future of Cameras
Movies, TV, stereoscopic presentations, VR, and the Internet are all part of
the same rapidly evolving media landscape. All the above is data. Zeroes
and ones. There is content creation software that mixes all of the above into
one or two connected programs, but it’s all just data that can be combined in
many ways. That’s why cameras are going to change. It’s already
happening.
The camera of the future will capture all the physical attributes of the
location where an image is recorded and save it as metadata. That means

the local weather conditions, orientation, and location of the camera in GPS
terms; a light probe of the location; a light-ﬁeld capture; and a 3D laser scan
of the location for site re-creation. Initially this will be 2–3 devices, but will
eventually end up in a single package. But even now, the several individual
technologies necessary for the camera of the future are being prototyped,
tested, and launched on Kickstarter as well as the labs of Sony, Nikon
Canon, Panasonic, Red Digital, and many others.
For example, drones are now being used to ﬂy around a location to make a
3D facsimile using computer vision. This provides a 3D location that is
usable by the visual effects team. Even indoors, a robot drone could be
programmed to ﬂy a pattern around a set capturing the 3D coordinates of all
the objects in a scene to be replicated later. This includes the surfaces of the
objects in the scene such as the materials used to make tables, chairs, and
carpets. Light probes are separate tools to record an HDRI sample of light
in a scene, but this could be captured by the drones or fed from a handheld
probe on the ground. Again, this is standard practice in visual-effects
cinematography as a non-camera activity, but it will all be moving into the
camera in the near future. The camera is the hub. The GPS coordinates,
time of day, and air temperature will all be on the same camera report,
which will be metadata recorded by the main camera.
Much of the world has been mapped from satellites and is available as GIS
Geography. GIS is Geographic Information Systems. Like video and sound,
the spatial coordinates and other information ends up as zeroes and ones
that can be used with GPS positioning data and fed into 3D animation
programs. Here the site and drone data captured for our future camera will
be used to create a 3D reconstruction of the set or outdoor location in the
scene. A model is made from this data and it can be matched back into the
recorded footage.
The advantage of this is the ability to re-light a scene in postproduction. In
the past, video and ﬁlm were color and gamma corrected after principal
photography. Digital Intermediates allow for much more control of color
correction, but re-lighting a scene dimensionally is beyond the practical
scope of traditional DI. However, with the full data capture described above
post photography will become the color correction of the future, allowing

for almost complete control over the image by including a great deal of 3D
data. Most of what I’ve described is available today in separate workﬂows,
but will eventually migrate to the camera as the ultimate data hub for live
action, while the 3D animation software will serve the same function in
animated and virtual production.
A recent development, Lytro, a light-ﬁeld camera, captures all the
traditional pictorial light values other cameras record, but adds the direction
of light to the mix. This allows depth of ﬁeld to be controlled after the
image is captured. Lytro understands the depth of the image, making green
screen unnecessary for effects since foreground objects that need to be
isolated are understood to be in a different depth plane than the background.
Currently, it is not a solution to replace existing cameras, but it shows how
without ﬁlm or even traditional digital sensor technology imagery can be
captured with huge advances in image control.
An appropriate way to end this chapter is with an extended example of
visualization using the storyboard from Empire of the Sun. This is a
continuation of the panels by production illustrator David Jonas included in
the last chapter. Apparent in every panel is Jonas’s ability to depict just the
precise moment in the action to convey the editing and dynamics of the
shots.
It is clear, however, that the storyboard serves to convey Steven Spielberg’s
visual style, and ﬁlmmakers familiar with his work will recognize some of
his favorite effects. For instance, the reﬂection of Jim’s face in the interior
car window appears as a semitransparent image through which we can see
the scene outside that Jim is looking at. This simple effect combines the
“shot, reverse shot” editing pattern in a single shot.
The sequence begins moments after the last one (page 78), which concluded
with the beginning of the Japanese invasion of Shanghai. Our new
storyboard begins when Jim’s parents level the hotel and try to escape the
city by car.

5 The Production Cycle
In this chapter, we’ll look at the production process, and how a 21st century
ﬁlmmaker can develop and present his or her vision to the creative team
through some well-deﬁned stages. The terms previs (previz), story reel, and
animatic are used interchangeably here, inspired by the preproduction
process of an animated movie that remains the most thorough process for
designing a movie.
Below are the steps in the production process for a live-action picture. This
is what you’ll be doing for 8–18 months on a Hollywood movie.
Scriptwriting is usually completed when a movie goes into production, but
I’ve included it here for writer/directors. For them, the creative arc from the
ﬁrst idea to the released movie is always based on the story. All the
production steps support the ﬁlmmaker’s singular focus.
• Scriptwriting
• Research
• Visual Script Analysis
• Production Design
• Rehearsal
• Cinematography
Anywhere from 10 to hundreds of artists and technicians work on a feature
ﬁlm, where 60-hour weeks are not uncommon. And then you’re done. The
sprint from inception to the screen is fast and intense. The director is faced
with thousands of decisions and what may seem like endless discussions
and conferences. The private time it takes to create becomes hard to ﬁnd.
Moviemaking is not a private process. Visualization can be the compass
you rely on if you lose focus in the overload of detail that a director is
expected to manage.
A detailed storyboard is useful in many ways, but in the past, its main
purpose was to convey to the crew what the director is looking for in the
ﬁnal ﬁlm. This is still an important use of previsualization, but with digital

tools, we can go way beyond storyboards and create something that is a
dress rehearsal for the movie.
Because many directors today are comfortable using graphic arts software,
they can create a visual presentation on their own before the movie is
greenlit. This predesign may even begin as a visual presentation to ﬁnd
investors for the movie years before production begins. The visual sales
presentation is called a deck, or the director’s vision book, or a pitch book.
But even with the director’s upfront work before production, once a movie
is up and running, it may make sense to hire a previs service company or
put together your own team in-house.
A slight detour into animation design is in order if only for a few
paragraphs, as this represents the gold standard for visualization of a motion
picture. As mentioned earlier, Disney organized and developed the modern
storyboard process, although he by no means invented storyboards or the
use of concept art. However, the Disney methodology is now used in all
animation studios as well as many visual-effects studios and on most
mainstream blockbusters.
Here’s what you need to know: the purpose of animation story reels is to
show a presentation that looks and sounds like your movie to an audience
before the movie is released. These presentations are carefully managed to
include audiences in the target demographic. Frequently, questionnaires are
used to record the reaction of each test audience participant. Some directors
such as animation director John A. Davis, creator, director, writer of
Nickelodeon’s Jimmy Neutron: Boy Genius, believe that sitting in a test
theater with an audience allows a director to quickly learn where the movie
fails to work just by observing the audience. When they are restless, they
are bored. After questionnaires are collected from the audience and
evaluated, it’s back to work to revise the areas where the audience did not
respond as expected.
Some ﬁlmmakers don’t like test screenings because they believe it exposes
the movie to corporate interference. This may be true, but a well-produced
previs version of the movie from beginning to end with dialogue, sound
effects, and music is hugely instructive as to what works and what doesn’t
even if you only screen the previs for the director and editor.

Scriptwriting
The screenplay has remained a remarkably consistent form, and the format
is a now-universal set of rules practiced around the world following a very
speciﬁc page layout. Agents, readers, and studios all expect the standard
form, but once a movie is funded, there’s no reason not to experiment with
a screenplay format that’s aimed at the crew of your movie.
What follows is an alternative approach to writing a story.
The following quote from David Byrne’s introduction to his published
screenplay and storyboards for the ﬁlm True Stories is a good description of
the way in which the visualization and writing processes can be combined.
The way this ﬁlm framework was constructed was inspired a little bit by my
work with Robert Wilson, by his working process. He often begins work on
a theater piece with mainly visual ideas and then layers the sound and
dialogue on top of that. I used a similar method. I covered a wall with
drawings, most of them representing events that could take place in one
town. Then I reordered the drawings, again and again, until they seemed to
have some sort of ﬂow. Meanwhile I assigned the characters inspired by the
tabloid newspaper articles to the people represented in the drawings.

David Byrne made drawings like this to develop his script for the movie
True Stories.

This approach is very personal and is dependent on the fact that Byrne was
the principal writer and the director of True Stories. His script was not
written in a style intended to convince a studio to buy the project, so he was
free to compose his story in a way that was more cinematic, putting visual
and verbal material on an equal basis. The ﬁlm is not a conventional
narrative (by Hollywood standards), and this is as much a result of the
compositional method as the cause of it. Observation is the key word.
Throughout the making of True Stories, Byrne sought material that was
strongly reportorial and placed it in his stylized framework. The point is
that the traditional approach to screenwriting, in which words precede the
image, is not the only way to write a script. On page 107 are some of the
storyboard panels drawn by David Byrne that helped shape the screenplay.
Research
If you’re a screenwriter you’ve probably done a fair amount of research, but
that was probably more for the story than the visuals. Depending on the
travel requirements, if any, scouting locations is research. Capture as much
photography and video as you can. Try and line up possible shots. If you
have an assistant, you can have them walk through the basic action of the
scene while you shoot. It’s a huge help when you are deciding between
more than one possible location for a scene. It will also be a big help to
anyone storyboarding the scene. It may even make its way into an edited
animatic. This raises the question of whether or not a senior storyboard
artist should go to a location. Typically, the producer, production designer,
and director are the key people who visit locations. If the cinematographer
is available he would also visit any key sites, but bringing a storyboard
artist on scouting trips is a relatively new development and by no means the
norm. It’s also not a bad idea.
There are two types of research. 1) Speciﬁc research about a place or
location you expect to feature in your movie and 2) information, images,
and sounds that are inspirational. Speciﬁc research would be ﬁnding images
of actual locations mentioned in your script; for example, the Grand
Canyon or Eiffel Tower. You will ﬁnd all the photos and illustrations of
these locations and save them to a folder on your computer. Inspirational
research might be a diner menu from the 1950s, when your movie takes

place. It won’t appear in the movie, but it evokes a feeling you want to
convey to the audience. You might ﬁnd photos of a time or place, for
instance newspaper advertisements from the period. You immerse yourself
in the subject and setting of your story to help you generate the most
evocative sights and sounds for your movie, but while it’s useful for you to
show this to the production designer, it also works if it’s just for you.
You could end up with a few gigabytes of video, photos, music, and sound,
but to prioritize them you might put them together as a collage in a pdf
booklet or an edited video presentation. How you present images will
change the way you respond to them. You can lay artwork out on the ﬂoor
or tack it to a wall. You will invariably move the images around, making
new juxtapositions that often generate new associations and ideas. Keep
going back to it. New ideas will emerge. Later, the production designer and
his or her team will be doing some of the same work, but the director will
search and select images in a way unique to him and hopefully fueled by
knowledge discovered while he did his own researching. Ultimately,
inspirational research encourages new ideas while speciﬁc research is done
to ﬁnd solutions to things found in the script.
Backstory
A subcategory of research is backstory. Research is real-world facts;
backstory is made-up facts. Backstory can also be part of the director’s
visual exploration.
Writers often develop backstories, or biographies, for their characters that
describe their lives before the time frame presented in the story. By creating
fully rounded characters with pasts, writers can create the present more
easily, predicting in a sense the behavior of characters whose history they
know. Photographs, pictures from magazines, newspaper headlines,
postcards, or a page photocopied from a high school yearbook can form a
visual backstory. These real-life details will help you imagine the grit and
texture of your story during the long hours spent typing on keyboard.
Here are some of the ways you can prepare the visual plan for your movie.
Visual Sketchbook

This begins with the research folder on your desktop containing images,
sounds, and notes. Now you’re going to add them into the screenplay on
blank pages, or you can create a book just for research. Either way the goal
here is to start making connections between ideas in the script and visual
material that is related to themes, locations, historical periods, character
arcs — everything in your script that is an adjective or noun that will
eventually be an image in a movie.
There are two reasons for all this. First is to identify what you’ve written in
the script with the actual thing in the real world. So if your story features a
restored 1967 Mustang, you’ll have one to show the production designer.
The second reason for doing this is for the less deﬁned aspects of the script.
Like the theme, or the mood of a time and place. So you might ﬁnd matchbooks from the 1950s, or an advertisement in a vintage newspaper. Maybe
all the images add up to a portrait of the pre-Civil Rights Era in the South.
This part of the process is less about showing speciﬁc things than about
generating a mood, and most importantly, searching for the small details
that help make an imagined or remembered time and place seem real. The
plot mechanics of Citizen Kane, for example, revolved, however contrived,
around the Rosebud sled. The sled was in the script, but that sort of visual
metaphor is often discovered when putting together the visual sketchbook.
You will also have researchers on even a low-budget movie, so the director
is not doing all the work while remaining the ﬁnal decision-maker on the
visual plan for the movie. To sum this up, the visual sketchbook is a way to
ﬁnd ideas as well as to clarify the visual choices you have already made.
VISUAL SCRIPT ANALYSIS
This comes in two ﬂavors: dramatic and visual. Dramatic analysis is
primarily for the actors and understanding the characters. I strongly
recommend Judith Weston’s books for directors and actors, Directing
Actors and The Film Director’s Intuition. These are the most thorough
books on script breakdown and analysis for actors and directors, but it’s not
about the visual design. Visual script analysis looks outside the script for
answers while dramatic analysis focuses on the text. But it’s their interplay
that’s interesting. Here’s a snapshot of how analysis generates visual ideas.

Your script has an opening scene at night. There is a lighthouse at the end of
a pier. To analyze this we need to ask questions, which is essentially the
Socratic Method.
Scene description
A woman, DREY, drives up to a lighthouse at night. A MAN is waiting for
her at the lighthouse. She has never seen the man before. They are meeting
to exchange information about the woman’s husband who has been lying to
her about his business trips. The man will show her pictures of her husband
in locations different than he claimed to be. Drey is just now ﬁguring out
she’s been lied to.
Analysis questions:
• What is the purpose of the scene?
• What does the main character in the scene want?
• What does Drey see when she gets out of the car?
• What does Drey expect to learn?
• What does she actually learn?
• What do you want the audience to learn? And when?
• What do you want them to feel?
Here’s what the answers look like:
What is the purpose of the scene?
To show that Drey is willing to investigate her husband. She no longer
trusts him.
What does the main character in the scene want?
She wants to ﬁnd out that her husband has not been lying.
What does Drey see when she gets out of the car?
She sees a man who she has never met. She sees an isolated meeting
place.
What does Drey expect to learn?
That if her husband has lied, he has a good reason, and it’s not about
her.

What does she actually learn?
She will learn her marriage is a sham. A front for something else.
What do you want the audience to learn?
That Drey is capable of defying her husband and therefore capable of
putting herself in danger.
What do you want them to feel?
Sympathy for Drey, but also fear for what she might do.
Now we need to look at how the visual language of the scene supports the
goals of the scene.
Here’s how adding visual information might look when we add it to our list
of questions:
What is the purpose of the scene?
To show that Drey is willing to investigate her husband. She no longer
trusts him.
Visual: She is walking into the wind, leaning forward. This shows the
effort she will make to get information. She’s determined.
What does the main character in the scene want?
She wants to ﬁnd out that her husband has not been lying.
Visual: She has her iPhone and is going to secretly tape the
conversation. This shows she believes the informant is not trustworthy,
but her husband is.
What does Drey see when she gets out of the car?
She sees a man who she has never met. She sees an isolated meeting
place.
Visual: It’s a long walk from the parking lot to the informant. The
informant is small in the distance alongside the huge lighthouse.
What does Drey expect to learn?
That if her husband has lied, he has a good reason to withhold
information.
Visual: Drey can be fumbling with the phone she expects to use to
record the converstation. She might inadvertantly swipe to pictures of
her family for a moment.

What does she actually learn?
She will learn that her marriage is a sham. A front for something else.
Visual: She is shown pictures that deﬁnitively establishes that her
husband leads a double life.
What do you want the audience to learn?
That Drey is capable of defying her husband and therefore capable of
putting herself in danger.
Visual: Drey asks for the pictures. This indicates she plans to use them
somehow and this can only produce conﬂict and danger.
What do you want them to feel?
Sympathy for Drey, but also fear for what she might do.
Visual: Create some mistake that Drey makes in her interaction with the
informant. This will make her vulnerable.
As you can see, not all the visuals have been worked out, but the process is
underway.
Here are some ideas that surface: Perhaps the informant remains concealed.
He wears a hoodie and sunglasses, and it’s night. This places Drey in a
much riskier situation. It’s a multiplier of the tension in the scene.
The lighthouse was originally written in the script as abandoned, but
perhaps it’s still functional. This allows us to have the spotlight on top of
the lighthouse rotate (that’s what they do). This provides a light that pans
over Drey and where she’s walking. Similarly, when she meets the
informant, he’s in almost complete darkness, but every 10–15 seconds, the
light pans over the scene again, but only for a few seconds. This allows you
to cut without cutting. The light, as it passes over the informant, reveals and
then goes away. So the light deﬁnes a momentary “shot.” The director has
an opportunity for Drey to be hyper-attentive when the light reveals small
details about the informant.
This is as far as we need to go here, but the same analysis process could be
applied to the color choices for the scene, camera placement, POV, music,
and sound effects.
Visual Script Analysis — Three Samples

Every ﬁlmmaker will devise his own way of working with the script before
shooting. Some ﬁlmmakers use storyboards while others write out a shot
list or an aerial view diagram of the location indicating camera setups.
There are many ways of turning the script into sequences, and your way of
working may vary with the project. The suggestions that follow represent
several methods of devising a shot plan or storyboard for a scene. They
could best be described as a series of daydreaming sessions based on, but
not restricted to, the script.
Let’s apply the different methods of planning shots to a hypothetical script,
Appomattox, based on the surrender of Robert E. Lee to Ulysses S. Grant at
Appomattox Court House at the end of the Civil War on April 9, 1865.
After reading the script many times and working on a ﬁnal polish with the
writer, the director has a general sense of how most of the scenes should be
staged. He has written notes in the margin of the script detailing speciﬁc
ideas. He ultimately transfers these to a separate notebook, which includes
photographs, sketches, and other reference material he has gathered. Later,
he visits Appomattox Court House and other location sites and takes
photographs at each location. Now the director is ready to begin working
out the shot-by-shot plan for each scene.
Creating a shot plan means describing the staging of the action, the size of
the shot, the choice of lens, and the camera angle. If the director uses a shot
list, it might read: “Wide shot, using a long focal-length lens to compress
the foreground and background. The camera is looking down on the Union
line from a modest high angle with trees in the immediate foreground. The
camera pans with a horseman who enters the right side of the frame.”
These are all traditional descriptions of the graphic quality and
photographic quality of a shot. The director, however, is ultimately
interested in how the photographic qualities of a shot determine the
narrative effect of the scene. The following exercise is a good way to
heighten your awareness of the relationship between visual and dramatic
elements by rephrasing traditional shot descriptions in dramatic terms. In
the four categories listed below, graphic qualities are matched with
equivalent narrative qualities, posed as questions.

1. Graphic: Where is the camera stationed?
Narrative: Whose point of view is being expressed?
2. Graphic: What is the size of the shot?
Narrative: What distance are we from the subject of the scene?
3. Graphic: What is our angle of view?
Narrative: What is our relationship to the subject?
4. Graphic: Are we cutting or moving the camera?
Narrative: Are we comparing points of view?
All these questions overlap somewhat, but answering them individually is a
technique that forces you to see the material in new ways. Now we can see
how they might be applied to the script excerpts from Appomattox.
Shot Plan — First Draft
After the director has visited the locations a second time and made
preliminary production choices based on the budget, he makes a shot plan
that is more than wishful thinking. He begins by taking the location photos
and any other research material and spreads it out where he works. Next, an
image list is made (not a shot list), based on the ideas that come to mind
when reading the script. He can write these on index cards, one image to a
card. This exercise amounts to free association and can include the
director’s feelings about the characters, how they are standing, what they
are looking at, or the look of the location. Sometimes a personal experience
comes to mind that is pertinent. For example, the director remembers losing
a ﬁght in elementary school and the sympathetic look on the face of a friend
afterwards. He writes a description of this potential shot on an index card.
Finally, the director includes speciﬁc shot or compositional ideas of
thumbnail sketches if he has drawing skill.
Second Draft
Now the director becomes more speciﬁc. We will use Script Sample 1
from Appomattox to show one way the director might work.
EXT. NORTHERN LINE AT APPOMATTOX CREEK — MIDDAY
We are behind the thin line of battle of the Northern troops.
They are remarkably quiet as the entire line of men watches

four horsemen under a flag of truce approach across an open
field. One of the horsemen is General Robert E. Lee. The line
slowly parts to let the riders pass. The soldiers turn and
watch Lee travel down the slope behind them into the trees that
line the creek. Several of the soldiers walk a short distance
down the slope to watch the General disappear into the mottled
sunlight of the forest.
The action describes the ﬁrst stage of Lee’s surrender to Grant. The lines of
battle during the Civil War were nothing less than the boundary between
victory and defeat, so when the Confederacy’s greatest general moves
behind the Union line it is an incredible moment. The Northern soldiers
know they are witnessing history and very probably the end of the Civil
War.
It seems to be the screenwriter’s intent in this scene to see the events from
the Northern soldiers’ point of view. This is a valid choice and answers the
ﬁrst of our four questions: Whose point of view is being expressed?
Next, we need to answer question two: What is the size of the shot — wide,
medium, or close-up? Or, in narrative terms, what distance are we from the
subject? This requires some clariﬁcation. I prefer to think of shot size in
terms of distance, since it discourages thinking of the shot size as a picture
frame surrounding the subject. Actually, the frame is a three-dimensional
box with the camera at one end. While we see the four sides of the frame,
we feel the distance to the subject. Because the camera is our point of view,
the size of the frame tells us how close we are to the action or subject. In
narrative ﬁlm, physical distance translates to emotional distance. When we
speak of shot size, we are really talking about our emotional relationship
with the action on screen.
Since the director has decided to make the point of view of this ﬁrst shot
that of the Union soldiers, the shot size/emotional distance refers to the
soldiers’ emotional separation from Robert E. Lee. He is at the dramatic
center of the scene. Though they may feel grudging admiration for him, and
even sympathy, until this moment he was their mortal enemy. For this
reason, the director’s choice of shot size/distance never comes closer than a
full shot of Lee as he rides past. He must remain inscrutable. The audience

must strain to see emotion on his face. To achieve this restraint, distance is
required.
Now the director considers the angle of view. Having determined the point
of view and distance, he knows that the camera will be placed alongside the
soldiers awaiting Lee’s approach. If the camera is at a low angle, it may
cause some practical problems because soldiers may block the camera’s
view. At the same time, the director considers the experience of the men on
the line to be communal since they are all intently focused on Lee and share
many of the same thoughts. Therefore, the camera should treat the soldiers
as a group. For both these reasons, the director believes the camera should
be at a medium high angle, perhaps 7–8 feet above the ground. In this way,
the camera has a clear view of Lee while encompassing many of the
soldiers in the shot.
Finally, the director comes to the question of editing (cutting vs. camera
movement) and whether or not the point of view should change. The
director’s sense of the action is that it should develop slowly as Lee appears
in the distance and moves towards the Northern line. There may be shots of
individual soldiers as the word spreads that Lee is approaching, but the
scene is solemn and should not be rushed. Therefore, a sequence shot on a
crane with the camera dipping down among the men for a close look before
moving up again as General Lee passes would be a good choice. With this
last decision, the director has determined how the scene will be shot.
This is how these decisions might be written in the shot list for script
sample 1:
“The camera is placed among the Northern troops on a crane or long jib
arm. Camera viewpoint is along the soldiers’ line of sight to Lee
approaching at a distance. The camera starts low among the men as their
attention turns to Lee approaching. The camera follows the group of men to
the line of battle. The camera slowly goes up to 8–10 feet as Lee passes by.
Pan with Lee and other horsemen as they pass. Additional shots: Shots of
men at various activities behind the line as they learn that Lee is coming.”
Figure 5.1 would accompany the notes.

As you can see, the schematic drawing can only roughly approximate a
dolly shot combined with a crane move upwards when we are dealing with
a sequence shot. The choreography of camera and the subject (timing of the
pan and the position of the camera when Lee passes, etc.) cannot be shown
without notes. The schematic, however, enables the production designer,
cinematographer, production manager, and assistant director to get cast,
crew, and equipment to the right place. This plan outlines all the problems
that the creative and organizational team needs to understand. This is our
ﬁrst plan for the script; now we can try another.
Figure 5.1
Moving on to Script Sample 2, the director shifts to another scene in a new
location.
EXT. FIELD — NORTHERN SIDE
We are on the side of the Union skirmishers who were on the
verge of clashing with Rebel pickets when a one-hour truce was
called. The advance line of greybacks are seated in a field at
the edge of a line of trees facing the blue skirmishers, who

are seated in and on both sides of a dusty road not quite 100
yards away. The men on both sides can see each other clearly.
One Union soldier sits near camera whiling away the time poking
his bayonet into an anthill.
This time the director doesn’t analyze the scene but instead writes out
camera instructions based on the action written in the script. He plans to cut
several shots together rather than use a long sequence shot:
“Low angle of rebels in a ﬁeld facing an equal number of bluecoats.
Camera is on the Union side shooting past several of the Union soldiers. In
the extreme foreground, a very young bluecoat passes the time by watching
an ant crawl up the bayonet of his riﬂe, which he has plunged into an
anthill. More and more ants come out. Across the ﬁeld, one of the rebels
nearest the young bluecoat is watching him from a distance of about 60
yards. The young bluecoat looks up and the two soldiers make eye contact.
Self-conscious, the bluecoat pulls his bayonet out of the ground, destroying
the anthill. He then shakes the ants off the bayonet. Cut to new shot from
behind the rebel. The rebel sees the bluecoat raise his gun, and thinking that
it is some kind of boastful gesture, picks up his own gun and lays it across
his lap. He looks straight back at the bluecoat, staring him down with a
smile.”

Figure 5.2: The schematic drawing at left uses numbers for each camera
angle in the sequence. The numbers correspond to the storyboard panels
that would follow this initial plan. The storyboard panels appear on pages
120 and 121.
Shot list:
1. Proﬁle of bluecoat alongside anthill. Rebel soldier in bg.
2. CU anthill and bayonet.
3. Reverse wide over the shoulder (OTS) of rebel soldier and bluecoat
in bg.
4 Over the shoulder (OTS) of bluecoat and rebel in bg.
Figure 5.2 shows how the schematic might appear for this scene.
Now we can move to Script Sample 3 and see how a dialogue scene might
be handled. This time we are in a farmhouse with General Lee.
INT. PARLOR — BRICK FARMHOUSE — AFTERNOON
General Robert E. Lee sits in a chair in a corner of the room.
He stares out the window.
Then realizing that he is staring, he begins to look around the
room, but this is nothing more than a form of pacing. Even this
is studied, and no action or emotion is shown that will betray
his feelings. His military secretary, Lt. Col. Charles
Marshall, a much younger man, looks at a picture on the wall,
taking his cue from his general to remain impassive. From time
to time Marshall looks to see what Lee is doing and finally
takes a seat by the fireplace.
Standing in the front hallway is Lt. Col. Or-ville Babcock of
Grant’s staff. He has left Lee and Marshall alone in the parlor
rather than make small talk, which is uncomfortable for all of
them. Babcock is greatly relieved to see General Grant and
General Sheridan enter the gate in the front yard. Babcock
opens the door and lets the officers in. Everyone stands.
BABCOCK

(to Grant)
General Grant—
Grant barely acknowledges Babcock. His thoughts are on only one
man. He goes directly across the parlor to Lee, who stands to
greet him. The two men shake hands.
GRANT
General Lee.
Grant looks around for a chair and Lee sits in his former
place. Grant is distracted for a moment as Babcock lets five
other officers into the room. They slowly file in and take
places around the room, and Marshall steps back to the
fireplace. Grant pulls a chair up and sits down directly across
from Lee. Grant tries to make the moment less tense for both of
them.
GRANT
I met you once before General Lee. I was stationed in
New Mexico, and you came to inspect the brigade. I
have always remembered your appearance and I think I
should have recognized you anywhere.
LEE
Yes, I know I met you on that occasion and I have
tried to recollect how you looked. But I have never
been able to recall a single feature.
This time the shot plan is not worked out in full. Since an interior scene
with dialogue and restricted movement is more easily staged with the
actors, descriptions consist of observations about the characters and how
they might be revealed with the camera. The shot notes might appear as
follows.
Shot plan:
1. Wide shot looking into room at Lee seated in the parlor of the
farmhouse. In the foreground is Lt. Col. Babcock, a member of
Grant’s staff, who escorted Lee through the lines. Standing in the

room near Lee is Lt. Col. Marshall, Lee’s military secretary. Babcock
is looking out the window, but he has also chosen to remain in the
hall and leave Lee alone with Marshall. Babcock looks in at Lee,
who is adjusting his glove. We hear voices outside. Offscreen,
Babcock straightens up.
2. Ext. Proﬁle LONG SHOT of house and front yard. Slightly low
angle. Grant is entering the gate with General Sheridan. He is
walking fast and brushing dust off his rumpled uniform in a lastminute effort to make himself presentable, but he doesn’t break stride
as he does so.
3. Int. Shot of Parlor. Camera is behind Lee at back of room. Lee has
stood up to greet Grant. Grant enters the front door and walks
straight to Lee. The two men shake hands. CUT TO:
4. REVERSE SHOT OF LEE AND GRANT as they shake hands.
Marshall crosses frame and CAMERA PULLS BACK. Grant looks
for a chair, Lee sits in his former place, and Grant pulls up a chair for
himself before Babcock can help him. Grant seems nervous. Lee is a
study in composure. CUT TO:
5. MED. SHOT OF FRONT DOOR. Camera height 5 feet. Union
ofﬁcers enter quietly as if attending a funeral. All steal glances of
Lee. Camera backs up with them, pans with one ofﬁcer to—
WIDE SHOT OF ROOM. ANGULAR PROFILE OF LEE AND
GRANT, FAVORING LEE. Camera height 4 feet. Hold this
throughout the entire conversation between the generals (this is the
master).
6. OTS LEE.
7. OTS GRANT.
8. CU LEE.
9. CU GRANT.

Figure 5.3: This storyboard began with the schematic in Figure 5.2 (page
117).
As you can see, there’s no strict form. Notes, diagrams, shot lists —
anything that clariﬁes what needs to be shot is useful. The only goal is to be
concise and to the point while including all the important visual detail.
Usually an overall description of the staging and visual action precedes the
numbered shots. In this way, the cameraman, actors, and crew will
understand the general plan and purpose for the staging, so that when the
numbered shots appear, the cutting and composition of the scene is clear.
Preparing a Storyboard
The shot plan the director just prepared is fairly precise. Now he’s ready to
turn this into a storyboard. One way to begin is to write out the shot plan in
storyboard boxes but without drawings. This forces the director to think in
sequence. For some reason, even a shot list seems to be more cinematic —
closer to what will be seen on the screen — if each shot description appears
within the borders of a frame. Storyboard frames of this type for the shot
plan beginning on page 119 are shown in Figure 5.3.
Next, the director adds drawings or photographs. This could be thumbnail
sketches or smartphone snapshots. Figures can be drawn over any photos.
The director has a supply of close-ups, medium shots, and long shots of the

principal actors. He uses these for his storyboard. He pastes the storyboard
panels onto individual pages and places them in a loose-leaf binder so that
the order of the shots can be changed. Figure 5.4 on page 121 is the type of
drawing the director might make at this stage.
Figure 5.4: The ﬁnal storyboard resulting from the script interpretation
process.
The director plays music while working with the storyboard and imagining
the scene. Different music suggests different ideas. When working with a
dramatic scene, the director might record rehearsal dialogue and play this
back while designing a scene.
It may take several ﬁlms to greatly improve your visual memory by
working with sequential art in this way, but your visualization skills will
ultimately be sharpened. Longer and more complex sequences will
eventually become easier to manage. You will ﬁnd that your ability to judge
what does and does not work (for you) has greatly improved.
Models and Miniatures
For a ﬁlm like Appomattox, models are particularly valuable. The ﬁrst scene
in our sample script, Lee crossing the Union line of battle during a truce,

involves hundreds of costumed extras, horses, and special props like
wagons and cannons. The logistical problem of staging such a scene is
enormous, and visualizing what goes on behind the camera is as important
as what the camera will record. The assistant director and the production
manager might want to include access roads, generator truck, crane, dolly
track, and any other equipment that will be a factor in staging the action.
Planning the ﬁrst scene of Appomattox, the director tries different groupings
of the miniature ﬁgures, photographing each of them with a still camera. Or,
he might go directly to a 3D app such as Frame Forge or Maya and have a
model of the location built, including soldiers, horses, and any other props
needed for the scene. With the landscape set up and lit for the correct time
of day, the director can easily move the camera around within the scene and
line up dozens of angles, exporting them as images for the storyboard in the
space of a couple of hours.
PRODUCTION DESIGN
The art department is set up during preproduction; and, depending on the
type of picture, its function is to build the sets, props, and costumes, and
integrate these with real locations into a cohesive whole. All this is done
quickly, so the director will be spending much of his time going to locations
or back at the production ofﬁces reviewing costumes and sets. Time to
research independently is over. You’re on the clock now. There will be a
production ofﬁce with rooms for the art departments, but nowadays,
painters and storyboard artists may work at home part-time.
When you begin, you will present any research you’ve done. It could be a
lot or a little. Over the course of a few weeks, the production designer will
show alternative approaches to the design, and bring in his or her own
research materials. Concept artists and storyboard artists will begin work
after being assigned scenes by the director. There may be several meetings a
week as the director gives feedback to the artists. There will be revisions
and searches for ideas. In addition to the scene-by-scene art decisions the
director and production designer must make there’s an ongoing search for a
visual logline. This is the feeling or idea that expresses the story’s core in a
visual way. After a few weeks or even months of designing, looking at
dozens of coffee table books and location scouting, key decisions are made.

You might bring in a previs team or create one in house. At this point, there
should be a solid foundation of approved locations and designs, you are by
no means ﬁnished with either of these, but you are well along. These are
shared with the previs team who will most likely attend story meetings.
Let’s say this movie has a $12 million budget; production may be only ﬁve
months. For a $110 million budget, preproduction might last a year or
longer. On a very low-budget picture, under $1.5 million, the director may
have to do much of his own planning, or not plan. There’s no budget for the
in-depth approach described above, but there would still be an art
department. The solution with micro budgets is often to shoot in a
documentary style.
One trend that is inevitable is virtual production design — the result of the
digital mixing of gaming technology, virtual sets, and digital effects. It’s a
natural evolution to combine real-time photorealistic game engines like
Unity and green screen keying to isolate foreground elements to preview
visual effects. Digital matte painting and digital sets have been in wide use
for 20 years, but more recently, the idea of creating a movie with actors
entirely in a studio with actors and a few props in front of a green screen is
being experimented with for use in both motion pictures and television
production featuring sci-ﬁ or fantasy worlds. Today there are many ﬁlms in
which a substantial portion of a movie was shot against a green screen.
Virtual sets have deﬁnite advantages over shooting in exotic locations, but
they are more expensive than a $10K matte shot with a still painting
standing in for a background location. Digital sets require an expensive
setup process, and coming up with seamlessly integrated digital and real
elements requires a large team of artists. However, this is the future. What
will change is that render times for digital elements will come down, and
many, many pre-made sets and locations will be for sale. This has already
begun, but in 20 years, the techniques now used by Hollywood blockbusters
will be commoditized. We can see this already, but prices and complexity
still need to improve. Today, virtual sets are designed the same way live sets
would be created, although many technical issues associated with live
action go away. The question is whether or not digital is equal to a live
location. Right now, the answer is that the top VFX studios in the world
such as ILM or WETA or dozens of smaller boutiques around the world can

create digital worlds that are indistinguishable from the real world. While
that’s 90% true of the best of the VFX pictures, there is an artiﬁciality to
most digital ﬁlm worlds that does not match the real world. It’s also a very
insular approach to ﬁlmmaking. A director gains inspiration and ﬁnds
evanescent details on location that no green screen studio shoot offers.
Production design has to be largely completed when principal photography
begins. Most movies are shot in 4–7 weeks, longer for bigger pictures, but
not much longer. The production designer and the art department remain in
place through production because things change, including losing a location
to bad weather. However, once cinematography begins, storyboarding is
reduced to a skeleton crew. But the handoff from production design to
cinematography begins at the start of principal photography.
CAMERA TESTS
The last part of production design, camera tests, were more common in the
past when ﬁlm was the standard photographic medium. In the 1930s, 40s,
and 50s, everything intended to be seen in the movie was subjected to a
