21 Transitions
The connections between shots are as important as the shots themselves and
usually signify changes in time and place in the narrative. Today, segues of
almost every type are used, with the greatest type of experimentation going
on in commercials and music videos. In all, there are only seven ways of
joining two shots together. They are:
• The cut
• The fade in
• The dissolve
• The white-in
• The wipe
• The white-out (or any colored version of this)
• The fade out
The Cut
Habit and convention have established that the dissolve indicates a passage
of time, while the cut is a present-tense segue. While this was an acceptable
generalization for most of the ﬁlms in the ’30s and ’40s, today there are
many more exceptions. The cut has undergone the greatest change, and is
used in much wider application in connecting different periods of time.
Television has popularized techniques that speed up the plot, and the cut has
become the connection of choice. One use that has become a favored
technique in television (now in features, too) is the montage sequence,
connected by cuts. Typically, the montage sequence covers hours rather
than days or weeks. Take, for example, the scene in Steven Spielberg’s
Jaws in which Ofﬁcer Brody, Captain Quint, and Hooper assemble the
shark cage onboard the ﬁshing vessel, the Orca. The scene is made up of six
shots each lasting 3 seconds for a total of about 18 seconds, compressing
what would probably take half an hour. In this case, the compositions are
composed angularly and record strong graphic contrasts in medium and
close shots, alerting us to the fact that normal, real-time continuity is being
interrupted. The result is a rhythmically compelling sequence that is

vigorous yet smooth. Another use of this technique, using a single camera
angle and composition, might show the construction of a house or the
growth of a family, with weeks and years foreshortened into a few seconds.
The result is time-lapse photography that can show months, years, or
decades passing within seconds.
The Dissolve
The dissolve, once approaching the status of a cliché, has begun to look
fresh again, having been replaced by the cut for many of its uses in recent
years. Regardless, it’s best if the ﬁlmmaker disregards fashion and employs
whatever technique does the best job in a given situation.
Because the dissolve can form a bridge between disparate times and places,
however shaky the logic of the connection, it has always been thought of as
a kind of Band-Aid for badly structured ﬁlms.
While the practice in the continuity style is to hide the cut, the dissolve is
intended to be seen or at least experienced. Dissolves can be any length but
usually last anywhere from approximately 1/2 second (10–12 frames) to
extremely expressive lengths of over a minute. The shortest dissolve
possible, and one rarely seen today, is the so-called soft cut, a dissolve
lasting only 1 to 10 frames. The soft cut was used in situations where a
transition required smoothing, such as nature footage in a documentary. For
example, the only available shots of a lion chasing a zebra might force the
editor to combine jarring, fast-moving shots to convey the basic action. A
dissolve could smooth the transitions but would be confusing and work
against the speed of the action. A straight cut would produce jumpy results.
In this case, the single-frame dissolve would be employed as a compromise.
It would soften the cut but not slow down the action. Usually the soft cut is
undetectable, and even most ﬁlmmakers cannot tell the difference. Its effect
is subliminal.
Focus-In/Out
In a focus-in/out, the tail of one shot loses focus until the image is
completely blurred and is dissolved to the next shot, which begins with a
new blurred image that is then brought into focus. Technically, it’s a
dissolve, but one that is concealed by another effect. Properly executed, the

dissolve is invisible because the two unfocused shots are joined at a point
where the images are too blurred to be identiﬁed. The loss of focus is
usually gradual, and the dissolve blends the two shots together as if they
were one. Of course, the viewer knows a bit of movie magic is taking place.
The effect has often been used as a POV shot, indicating loss of
consciousness. One version you’ve probably seen many times is the patientin-an-operating-room shot. We share the patient’s viewpoint as he is
anesthetized and the operating room becomes a blur. Slowly the picture
returns to sharpness, and we are in a recovery room hours later.
The Match Shot
Match shots are two adjacent shots that share a graphic element that is
registered identically in each. Though described in many screenplays as a
match cut, most often a dissolve links the shots. The dissolve helps to
smooth out any imperfections in registration and lets the viewer enjoy the
change more thoroughly. But this is not always the case. In À bout de
soufﬂe (Breathless), Jean-Luc Godard makes a match cut on Jean Seberg as
she drives in a convertible with the top down. Suddenly the background
changes from day to night though the car and driver remain in the same
position — a very radical cut in its day. Interestingly, it combines two very
different qualities: the disruptive time shift of the jump cut and the graphic
smoothness of the match cut.
Wipes
A traditional wipe is rarely seen today unless the ﬁlmmaker wants to
deliberately recall the Hollywood style of the ’30s and ’40s. In its most
familiar form, the wipe is the cross-frame movement of a new shot over an
old one and resembles a curtain being drawn. George Lucas used angular
wipes in Star Wars — an homage to the serials made by B studios like
Monogram and Republic, which inspired the movie.
Wipes can move in any direction, vertically, horizontally, or diagonally
across the frame. Circles, squares, and spirals — any conceivable shape —
can be used to remove one shot and introduce a new one. A wipe can be
shaped to resemble a desired design element like a police badge or a
keyhole expanding or contracting until one shot has replaced the other.
Flying Down to Rio (1933), the movie that introduced the team of Fred

Astaire and Ginger Rogers, contains dozens of different wipes used
throughout the course of the movie to combine scenes.
Another variation of the wipe, the push-off or push-over, has the incoming
shot “pushing” the outgoing shot from the frame so that it appears to be
moving intact across the frame. This differs from the traditional wipe, in
which the new shot appears to move over the one it is replacing.
A wipe can also be combined with an element in the shot moving in the
same direction and at the same rate — for example, a telephone pole or a
tree. In this case, the leading edge of the wipe is matched to the movement
of the pole or tree trunk. Cars, joggers, or an elevator door, anything
moving across the frame, can motivate a wipe of this kind. A version of this
type of wipe used in cartoons has an animated character pulling the new
shot into place. A recent car commercial shows an outgoing shot of a car
“torn” from the screen in strips as if it were paper, revealing a perfectly
registered car of a different color underneath.
The Action Wipe
The action wipe lies somewhere between an optical wipe and a match cut,
and is made in the camera or, to be more speciﬁc, in the editing room. The
action wipe connects two shots in a scene rather than linking two scenes in
different locations. In almost every case, this effect is used to move from a
wide shot to a medium or close shot, making the cut smoother by what
amounts to an “invisible” cut on action. What distinguishes this cut from a
normal cut on action is that the action is not of the principal subject.
Instead, some design element within the frame that moves between the
camera and the principal subject provides the action. The element can be
any object positioned close enough to the camera to completely block the
lens while moving through the shot. Typically, the action would be shot
twice with the movement the same in each shot and the angle of view
identical, but with a change in frame size. Later the two shots would be
connected at the point where the subject is blocked from our view. In recent
years, action wipes have also been used to connect shots taken from
different viewing angles and even different periods of time (Figure 21.1).

Figure 21.1: The action wipe.
As with the match shot, which is similar to the action wipe, a cut or dissolve
can connect the shots in an action wipe. Usually a dissolve is used to make
the effect as seamless as possible.
Transformations
In special cases, the transformation of the principal subject in a shot can be
considered a transition. Strictly speaking, the transformation is not the
joining of two separate shots — the deﬁnition of a transition — but there is
a complete change of subject. Figure 21.2 is a series of transformations by
the pioneer animator, Émile Cohl, from 1909.
Up until recently, this type of effect was so unusual that there was no reason
to consider it anything but a novelty. Computer graphics, however, are now
able to execute far more complex transformations between photographic
images relatively easily. Portions of the frame, or the entire picture, can be
broken down and reconstructed into a new photographic image smoothly
with great textural and dynamic variation. Effects for TV commercials are
designed for maximum impact to hold viewer attention. This is very nearly
the opposite goal of transitions in the continuity style, but computer
graphics are capable of any sort of image conﬁguration if the time and

energy are spent to execute it. We can expect much more of this in the
future.
Fades
The fade to black and the fade up from black have the effect of setting
episodes off from one another like chapter headings. In this respect, the
purpose of the fade is fundamentally different from the dissolve or cut. The
fade separates scenes, while the dissolve and cut connect scenes. Going to
black and then coming out of black to picture is a complete departure from
the narrative, though if it is done rapidly the pause is minimal.
A fade can employ pictorial elements in the scene. A typical use of this idea
would be a scene in a room that ends with the lights being turned off. While
still in black, the cut is made to the next scene, which could begin with a
train coming out of the darkness of a tunnel.
White-In/White-Out/Color
Fades can be made to and from any color or made to white, an effect that
John Huston used for the closing shot of Prizzi’s Honor. The movie ends at
a bright sunny window that slowly becomes blindingly white until the
frame is completely bleached out. This is the end of the ﬁlm, so we don’t
return to another scene, but obviously all that would be needed to do so is a
white-in. This can be motivated by any bright element in the new shot like a
bright sky or lamp. The white-out has a particularly ethereal quality, though
there is no real convention attached to the mood it produces, unlike the
dissolve, which tends toward the lyrical and elegiac.
The Freeze Frame
This effect differs from the other types of visual punctuation we’ve looked
at in that it is usually employed as a period rather than a transition between
scenes. The best-known use of the freeze frame is in Truffaut’s Les Quatre
Cents Coups (The 400 Blows) in the memorable ﬁnal shot of Antoine
Doinel alone on the beach. The elegiac quality of the shot, the sense of
ﬁnality and inescapable fate became a favorite effect in the ’60s and was so
overused that it has almost completely disappeared. More recently, the
freeze frame has served a more practical use as a species of POV when used
to show the viewpoint of a photographer taking a picture. Usually we are

watching some action when a burst of white and the sound of a shutter
closing indicate a strobe ﬂash and the moment of exposure. The action
freezes momentarily, showing us the photo that the photographer desires.
The effect was used well in Robert Altman’s M*A*S*H when Hawkeye and
Pierce break in on an ofﬁcer who is shacked up with a Japanese girl in order
to take compromising pictures. We see the pictures as a series of freeze
frames as the ofﬁcer madly scrambles to pull on his trousers.
Montage
“Montage” is a problematic term. To the Europeans all editing is montage;
to the early Soviet ﬁlmmakers, Kuleshov, Pudovkin, and Eisenstein, it
meant their special brand of associative editing; or, in the words of
Eisenstein, “Montage is an idea that arises from the collision of independent
shots.”
In the United States and Great Britain, montage has its own special
meaning: a brief sequence of linking devices, usually dissolves, used to
convey the passage of time or a series of locations. It is this transitional use
that is of interest to us here.
Actually, montage of this type is not so much a linking device as a
condensed narrative, a form of visual shorthand that uses actual transitions
(dissolves, cuts, fade-ins/outs) in rapid succession to link ideas. Montage
frequently uses symbolic images to represent change — for instance, piles
of coins and dollars that grow larger dissolved against images of the stock
exchange and industry to show the ﬁnancial rise of a character. Slavko
Vorkapich, an immigrant from Yugoslavia, became known as a montage
specialist in the United States during the late silent and early sound periods
creating these types of sequences. Vorkapich, a gifted ﬁlmmaker, was
interested in experimental ﬁlms and the avant-garde ﬁlm movement in
Europe. Like other ﬁlmmakers experimenting with new techniques, he
found work creating new versions of standard narrative sequences depicting
dreams, drunkenness, after-death experiences, altered states, and fantastic
realms.
The montage is not used much today. When a long period of time is
condensed to a short sequence, cuts are preferred to dissolves. Montage that

conveys an idea or concept, as in the pile-of-money example, is rarer still,
leaving an opportunity to experiment with old techniques in new ways.
Split-Screen Effects
Actually, the split-screen is not exactly a transitional effect, though it is
used to join images that would otherwise be seen in separate shots. In 1927,
the French director Abel Gance used the technique in several sequences in
his epic biography, Napoléon, turning the screen into a triptych. He called
this early widescreen process Polyvision. Three projectors were used to
obtain the necessary width for panoramic shots, but they could be used in
any combination, each showing a different image or a center image ﬂanked
by two identical shots. Occasional use of the split-screen was used in the
silent era to show two sides of a telephone conversation simultaneously, and
a related technique, created with a multi-image lens, multiplied a single
image in a revolving circular pattern. This became a standard representation
for hallucinations and nightmares. Later, Busby Berkeley would use this
device for purely graphic design.
Split-screen effects were never widely used and all but disappeared by the
’40s. The technique was resurrected in the ’60s in John Frankenheimer’s
Grand Prix with the screen split into varying patterns of mortices. Anyone
familiar with multi-image slideshows would recognize the technique, and
though this type of multimedia presentation has continued to develop and
thrive in corporate and advertising communication, there has been no
crossover to narrative ﬁlm.
The only director who has continually experimented with the split-screen is
Brian De Palma, who has made a genuine effort to use the effect to discover
new ways of telling a story. The effect is usually done as an optical at a lab
after the ﬁlm has been shot, but for compositional reasons it is probably
best to compose the frame with the split-screen in mind. Simple split-screen
effects can also be created in any camera that can be accurately backwound, and are therefore well within the means of independent ﬁlmmakers.

22 Format
The Frame
Painters, graphic artists, and photographers are able to vary the size of their
work surface to suit the subject, from life-size murals to locket-size
portraits, even though the viewpoint is ﬁxed. The ﬁlmmaker ﬁnds himself in
the opposite situation. He can vary his viewpoint within a ﬁlm but not the
shape or size of the frame that encloses it. He does have a choice, however,
in the format he uses.
The proportions of the ﬁlm frame are called the aspect ratio, a description
of the relationship between the horizontal and vertical dimensions of the
picture. The aspect ratio basically determines the shape of the image, which
is almost always a rectangle of varying proportions, depending on the
format.
A Brief History of Formats
George Eastman ﬁrst made stripﬁlm available in the 1890s, and since then
there have been over one hundred different systems for photographing and
projecting motion pictures. In the silent era, while the studios were just
beginning to monopolize the making and distribution of motion pictures,
there were a variety of formats, though the predominant aspect ratio was
1.33:1. The conﬁnes of this rectangle were not ﬂexible enough for
ﬁlmmakers like D.W. Grifﬁth, who occasionally masked a portion of the
frame horizontally or vertically to compose speciﬁc scenes. More common
temporary maskings of the frame included iris effects that turned the screen
into a small soft-edged circle or oval, an effect similar to a close-up for
directing our attention to a point on the screen. The most impressive format
experiment in the silent period was the Polyvision process used for Abel
Gance’s Napoléon. This was mentioned in the previous chapter because of
its use of split-screen effects, but these compositional devices were not the
purpose for using three projectors or widening the screen. Gance’s main
interest was in providing a monumental viewing experience that
overwhelmed the audience. While the screen could be stretched to include

Napoleon’s armies, German director F.W. Murnau preferred to condense the
screen width to a square for a smaller ﬁlm like the lyrical Sunrise (1927).
Despite these and other experiments, the 1.33:1 proportion remained the allpurpose format throughout the silent period based on a ﬁlm gauge of 35
mm. In 1932, the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences made this
the standard aspect ratio, and, because they now had sound to consider, took
some of the available width of the frame for the optical track. To maintain
the 1.33:1 ratio, the height of the frame was lowered, and the result was the
Academy aperture, with an actual ratio of 1.37:1. This was the standard for
nearly 20 years.
In the 1950s, the motion-picture industry responded to the economic threat
of television with a whole array of widescreen systems designed to entice
people out of their living rooms and back into the theaters. This was a
repeat of a similar trend way back in the late ’20s and early ’30s, when the
sound era began. In fact, many of the widescreen formats adopted in the
’50s were developed or suggested two decades earlier. Among these were
several ﬁlm gauges that were brieﬂy in use, including the Paramount
process (56 mm), Spoor Natural Vision (63 mm), and Fox Grandeur (70
mm). These and other innovations — stereophonic sound for one — might
have become standardized in the ’30s had it not been for the Depression,
which caused the studios, who originally backed the changes, to rethink
their plans. Motivated by more conservative ﬁnancial expectations, the
studios abandoned the costly replacement of existing equipment.
Twenty years later, when television threw down the gauntlet, most of the
technical preparation for widescreen motion pictures had already been
worked out. Not that the technological problems were particularly difﬁcult;
motion-picture cameras and projectors represent nineteenth-century
technology (essentially mechanical), and while there have been reﬁnements,
no major breakthroughs have been made in the basic design of the camera
or projector, with the exception of the IMAX rolling loop system. Some of
the widescreen systems developed over the last four decades are: Cinerama,
originally a three-camera, three-projector system similar to Abel Gance’s
Polyvision; Todd-AO; CinemaScope; VistaVision; Techniscope; Super
Panavision 70, and Ultra Panavision 70. Only a few of these systems are in
use today, and a mere list of trademarks does not begin to cover the

complex subject of available ﬁlm and camera systems until the mid-2000s,
when digital cameras and projection changed everything. As of this writing,
ﬁlm is still in use for shooting theatrical features; however, digital is for all
practical purposes the dominant technology for commercial ﬁlmmaking.
The development of digital cameras and projection formats has been
inﬂuenced by, and closely follows, the traditional ﬁlm formats that directly
preceded it. Until fairly recently, there were three ﬁlm proportions in
general use: 1.85:1, 1.66:1, and 1.75:1. In the United States, 1.85:1 was the
unofﬁcial standard, while 1.66:1 was popular in Europe. Of the smaller
formats, 16 mm, Super 16, and Super 8 were widely used, utilizing aspect
ratios hovering around the 1.33:1 ratio. 16 mm can be used in the Academy
and widescreen formats, which was the original reason for the introduction
of Super 16 in the early 1970s. Super 16 uses more of the available negative
and, therefore, produces a sharper, less grainy image.
Digital Acquisition
Movies can be ﬁlmed but can also be made entirely in the computer without
a physical camera in sight, and so the popular term “digital acquisition” was
born. Whether real or virtual, digital cameras output frames, the equivalent
of the ﬁlm negative in sizes measured in pixels. Shorthand for various
formats are names like 2k and 4k or image sizes that are 2,000 or 4,000
pixels in size respectively. The most common format is 1920 × 1080 with
an aspect ratio of 16:9, which is almost identical to 1.85:1. The aspect ratio
is independent of the resolution so a 16:9 image can be composed of a few
thousand pixels or millions of pixels. That’s where the notion of megapixels
(a term often mentioned in the still-camera world) originated. The 2k and
4k image sizes mentioned above are 2 megapixels and 8 megapixels. The
math is simple: the megapixel size of a digital image is found by
multiplying the pixel width by the height of the image. A 2k image is 2048
× 1080 pixels, or 2,212 million pixels when multiplied. This is rounded off
to 2 megapixels for simplicity. A 4k image is 4096 × 2160 or 8,857,360
pixels, which is rounded off to 8 megapixels.
While we used to speak of ﬁlm resolution in terms of the size of the
negative such as 16 mm, 35 mm, or IMAX, the digital equivalent of the ﬁlm
negative is the camera sensor. Their size is referred to in terms of

megapixels, and basically represents the level of detail captured in each
image. Professional still cameras such as Nikon, Canon, or Sony DSLRs
have cameras with sensors in the 12-megapixel to 50-megapixel range. In
motion pictures, the current crop of professional cameras are in the 4megapixel to 8-megapixel range. This number is always going up, and there
is no reason not to capture more and more information as time goes on. The
driver here is the human need to replicate reality.
Digital Projection
Beginning in the early 2000s, the major US studios made the primarily
ﬁnancial decision to begin the biggest infrastructure change in the delivery
and presentation of motion picture since movies began. Digital “prints”
have replaced ﬁlm in 98.2% of cinemas worldwide as of May 3, 2016.
Digital prints don’t wear out compared with a ﬁlm print that remains
pristine through only a few dozen showings before the delicate emulsion
surface is scratched or chipped. Digital prints can be rapidly produced and
are far less expensive than ﬁlm prints. Digital projection does not have gate
jitter, the microvibration seen by the audience as a high-frequency image
movement, a consequence of mechanically transporting ﬁlm and
magnifying the frame several thousand times. Unlike a ﬁlm print, a digital
print is sharp across the entire screen when projected. This is not possible
with mechanical ﬁlm mechanisms. However, there remain ﬁlmmakers such
as Quentin Tarantino who are trying to preserve the traditional ﬁlm
experience in arthouses. At the time of this writing, while ﬁlm is still used
for some feature ﬁlms at the insistance of a few A-list directors, digital
dominates. In 2015, approximately 85% of the 100 top-grossing movies
were shot digitally. Sadly, in the near future, silver chemistry and the beauty
of a ﬁlm print will only be found in specialty presentations and venues.
Technically, 16:9 (1.77:1) is the international format of HDTV and Full HD.
This is primarily a television and computer-monitor standard, but has
become an alternative to 1:85 in the theaters. Typically, the dimensions of
16:9 in consumer and prosumer cameras is 1920 × 1080 pixels, even if it’s
captured at a higher resolution and downsampled. But this was barely
established in the early 2000s, when 4k and higher resolutions were

introduced, including 4k UHD, 5k UHD, and 8k UHD. Expect greater
resolution and higher frame rates.
At the same time, digital resolution is evolving, and frame rates are being
tested far beyond that of 24 frames per second (fps), the sound ﬁlm standard
for nearly a century. Television is a bit more complex, but uses a 30fps rate
derived from 60 ﬁelds per second. New frame rates of 48fps and 60fps have
been suggested for years, and recently frame rates of up to 120fps are in
development. This is a controversial subject, with the eventual goal of
replicating reality with higher frame rates versus the traditional look of
ﬁlms projected at 24fps. The so-called “live” look of higher frame rates as
seen in soap operas and newscasts is off-putting for many viewers. One of
the best descriptions of the perceptual difference between high and low
frame rates is that ﬁlm (24fps) is past tense and television (30fps) is present
tense. When viewed, 24fps seems to be an experience that has happened in
memory while faster frame rates tell us the presentation is happening as we
watch. There is debate whether the preference by some people for 24fps is
or is not a conditioned response, the result of seeing the slower frame rate
for nearly a century. Expect this and other experiments in formats to
continue. The new digital projectors in theaters can be reprogrammed in
many ways that were impossible with ﬁlm projectors, allowing for more
experimentation with formats.
Stereoscopic
The digital projection infrastructure transformation coincided with and
made possible a widespread comeback of stereoscopic viewing, which now
appears to be short-lived. The studios could charge more for a stereoscopic
presentation and so this became part of the upgrade to digital in most
cineplexes, including the need to provide reusable eyewear. Similarly, the
TV manufacturers got behind the new technology and began making 3D
ﬂat-screens. However, despite an initial interest from the mainstream
audience the popularity of stereoscopic viewing has dropped off
signiﬁcantly. Why?
While the technical virtues of digital projection allowed for improvements
in both the capture and projection of stereoscopic ﬁlms, the required glasses
have never been popular. Far from unobtrusive, either shutter glasses or

polarized light ﬁltration reduce screen brightness for the viewer. It’s
noticeable even to the casual viewer. The cost of shooting or postprocessing stereoscopic is not inexpensive, and contrary to what has been
said by various promoters of stereoscopic movies, it does not signiﬁcantly
add to or change the fundamentals of continuity style ﬁlmmaking. In fact,
the rapid loss of interest in stereoscopic movies is partially the victim of the
human visual system. Namely, adaptation. Unless the stereoscopic
experience exaggerates the effect of depth in shots that seem like stunts, the
brain successfully reduces the effect. It does this because the various
processes needed to fool the eye are unnatural and tax our viewing system.
For most shots and staging, the stereoscopic effect diminishes once the
movie begins. As the neuroscience of perception has still not produced a
comprehensive understanding of vision, the nature of the perceptual
experience of stereoscopic viewing is unknown. As both a ﬁnancial
opportunity and a useful innovation, stereoscopic has failed to deliver.
On the following pages are comparisons of ﬁlm formats and digital formats.
While ﬁlm formats are a matter of history, the digital formats in the
following diagram are part of a constantly (and rapidly) evolving science of
image capture and presentation. It’s guaranteed they will be quite different
within ten years. Finally, the list of ﬁlm and digital formats is not
comprehensive. What follows is a general introduction to formats, laying
out the major trends.
digital formats

23 Shadowing
This section includes a quick start for new directors staging actors on set
without storyboards or previs to consult. First, let’s shadow a director.
Shadowing is the term given to the practice of a director-in-training being
allowed to follow an actual director for a few days while ﬁlming. So you
just got the call sheet outlining the day’s schedule. We’ll start with a short
description of a director’s typical day and then we’ll look at a series of ideas
to expand your staging chops. These are ideas based on staging examples
from earlier chapters, but packaged as simple solutions you can easily
remember. Let’s go. It’s 7:00 AM and you need to be on set.
The movie where you will shadow the director is shooting at one location
(multiple rooms in a suburban home). The scene involves ﬁve actors. It’s a
simple setup. Two police ofﬁcers come to a home where a 911 call was
made by a teenaged girl to stop her parents from ﬁghting. There’s no
violence and no real reason to call 911 but the girl has nowhere else to turn.
Of course when the police arrive, the teen’s parents are furious at her.
This has all been worked out in a storyboard based on a speciﬁc home
secured as the location, but at the last moment, a new house is substituted.
The layout of the new home is so different that the storyboard showing long
dolly moves is not going to work. The director won’t see the new location
until he arrives there early on the day of the shoot, requiring him to
improvise.
A day of ﬁlming with a professional crew follows the following basic steps:
Blocking, lighting, rehearsal, and shooting.
7:00 AM
Crew arrives. After grabbing coffee and a donut at the craft services
table, the grips and cinematographer (DP) begin to scope out the
interiors and what windows need to be blocked to keep out sunlight.
The sun will be replaced by artiﬁcial lights. Equipment is unloaded
from trucks and staged in the driveway and garage. Set dressing begins
for areas the crew knows they will have to shoot. However, they need

the director to explain the basic blocking of the scene now that the
storyboards are only partially accurate.
The director, cinematographer, production manager, and line producer
now need to ﬁnalize the staging areas. Originally, the open ﬂoor plan of
the old location had wide openings between the kitchen, the front door
and entranceway, and the living room. This provided room for the
tracking shots. Conversely, the new location is made up of separate
rooms connected by narrow hallways and doors that are not dolly
friendly, and this will add a lot of time to the setup. The line producer
asks what is most important to the director: the tracking shots or
keeping the teen hidden in the kitchen when the police arrive, where she
can observe without being seen. The director agrees to give up the
tracking shots. He’s most interested in the teen being able to observe the
police and her parents secretly. The director, cinematographer, line
producer, and production manager begin walking through the scene and
lining up shots. Often, actors’ stand-ins are used at this point. The
director will use a director’s ﬁnder (a lens simulating what the camera
will see) as they walk through the action of the scene. They begin to see
technical issues with each new choice they consider.
The typical limitations the crew must deal with include where to hide
lights and where to lay dolly track. Film crews are amazing at making
almost any directorial request possible, but it’s always a matter of time
and money. Lighting for a tracking shot may take more time than for a
locked-off shot, and the line producer may tell the director they will
have to limit some other part of the plan. The original plan had the teen
on the ﬁrst ﬂoor and the parents on the second ﬂoor. Now the director
sees that he can put the teen upstairs observing from the hallway
looking down to the front door when the police arrive. The prop master
will move a large indoor plant from the patio up to the balcony so the
girl has a place to hide when the police arrive.
A few other decisions of this kind are made and it’s now 8:15 AM. The
director goes over the script and new shot list with the assistant director,
script supervisor, and production manager. These professionals will ask

detailed questions about every change in the scene so that story
continuity is maintained.
8:45 AM
The actors arrive. They are met by the director and sent to makeup. The
plan is to get to a ﬁrst shot by 10:15.
9:30 AM
The new shot order is conﬁrmed and requires bounce lighting into the
front door area. The ﬁrst shots will be the police arriving. The front of
the house is cleared and a rented squad car positioned in front of the
house. The director and DP now discuss more details of the shot. A new
problem arises. The squad car is not visible from the best camera angle
for the front door because there’s a tree in the way. The director and the
cinematographer ﬁnd a solution that requires a camera move. While this
is being discussed, the line producer tells the cinematographer the tree
can be digitally removed and a digital squad car added, but it will cost
$5,000. Including the squad car in the shot is not critical to the story so
the idea is scrapped. No squad car, just the police at the front door.
10:15 AM
The two actors playing the police are in uniform and the actors playing
the teen’s parents arrive after makeup. Everyone is in costume and
ready for rehearsal. The director likes to give very little direction
initially; he wants the actors to perform as they would if they were in a
real situation. The director wants to see how everyone will interact. He
hopes to observe some unscripted natural behavior from his actors that
he can ask them to repeat. The director tells the actors to only give 50%,
and to just walk through the scene. They do this with the camera setup
and because it’s a digital camera, they record the rehearsal. After a few
rehearsals, the director has some new ideas. The father of the teen in the
movie goes to answer the door, but stops partway in the foyer when he
sees the police through the window. He looks up at the balcony where
the teen is hiding. Her bedroom is on the second ﬂoor. This is not in the
script, but the director thinks this hesitation is great. It tells the audience
that when there’s trouble the father instinctively suspects his teen

daughter is involved. This means adjusting the dolly move to capture
this new moment.
10:45 AM
Everyone wants to get the shot done before the noon lunch break. After
a few moments’ dolly rehearsal, they shoot the ﬁrst take. With time
taken for coverage, the scene is completed by noon. This includes a
second handheld camera taken up to the second ﬂoor, where the teen’s
POV shots are recorded with the two police ofﬁcers and the teen’s
parents in the shot. These are shot during rehearsal because lip synch
won’t be seen at this distance. Distance and dialogue can be added later
in postproduction.
1:00 PM
After lunch, the new shot is being setup. This is the ﬁght that sets off the
whole scene. It was supposed to be shot in a bedroom, but the director
now has the scene staged in the kitchen. The director begins working
out the blocking with the cinematographer and they decide they can
start the scene in the porch, following the father into the kitchen, where
the argument with his wife begins. The director rehearses the actors
playing the parents into the kitchen and lets them walk through the
scene. Again, he tells them not to give him their full emotional intensity.
The teen’s reaction to the ﬁght while in her bedroom will be shot on
another day. The teen’s bedroom will be a stage set since it is used in
several scenes, justifying the expense of the build.
The rehearsal in the kitchen is going well, but it’s centered around the
kitchen table and seems too contained for the director. He wants to show
the wife’s impatience so he asks the actress to respond by using more
space and not standing so close to the husband. The director points to
two moments in the scene when the wife can just walk a few yards
away in frustration and turn her back on her husband. He tells her to
play it like she’s a caged animal. He’s building the case that the wife
wants to leave the relationship, but has not acknowledged that to herself
yet. So shaking her head and just walking out for a moment is stronger
than the words in the script. The director now has the staging he wants,
but the lighting has to be adjusted since the actress walks over to a

window. There are a couple of tricky camera moves to make this work.
There is now a tech rehearsal with the stand-in actors.
2:30 PM
The actors come back and do a couple of camera rehearsals, but the
timing is tricky. They shoot the rehearsal and look at it. There are some
modest adjustments and a line of dialogue is changed. Now everyone is
ready to go for the shot.
This is a longer scene than the front door scene and takes a couple of
hours to complete. The actors are given a short break to refresh since
the scene is emotionally draining. The director would like more time,
but the actors are losing their energy. The earliest takes were best. The
footage is reviewed by the director and producer and they feel they have
what they need.
4:30 PM
There are some pickup shots they can shoot with the wife taking trash
bags to the garage, and these shots are shot with a handheld camera
outside. Meanwhile, the next big scene is back in the foyer when the
teen’s father calls her downstairs. This is a relatively short scene
because the girl does not come down. She climbs out the window of her
bedroom. The father will go upstairs, but that’s the last moment to be
shot in the house. The story now moves to the teen’s escape.
5:30 PM
Since the foyer was already lit, it only takes about 20 minutes for the
new setup to be ready for the rehearsal. The cast is brought back in. The
scene takes 30 minutes to shoot. The cast is let go. However, the
director and DP don’t wrap quite yet. They get coverage of details in the
house and potential moody cutaways and POV shots of the interiors.
The exterior of the house will be a different home in another city.
7:00 PM
It’s a wrap. The crew begins breaking down the gear.
This is a fairly typical day’s shooting of simple scenes. An action or VFX
picture will have many scenes with all the same story and actor concerns,

but far more technical considerations. The quiet time with the actors is
harder for the director to ﬁnd.
A director has many things on his mind when directing, but when it comes
to blocking, once the actors move through the scene more or less how they
would act if they weren’t ﬁlming, the director has to take reality and make it
work for the camera. There are a few basic techniques the director can rely
on to help do this. We’ve already discussed them, but while explaining them
in detail is necessary, the following short cuts boil them down to very
simple concepts.

24 Short Cuts

basic coverage
All the action takes place in a very conﬁned space; essentially, a ﬁvefoot circle typical of televison staging for many programs. This
approach is fast to light and the background does not need as many
extras or set dressing. The downside is that this staging does not help
convey the sense that the action is happening in the real world.
In the following quick ﬁxes, we’ll look at ways to expand and
improve the basic coverage we’re using here.

walking & talking
Our base coverage is staged in a ﬁve-foot circle where the dialogue is
spoken. We looked at a variation having Anne approach from a
distance. We looked at a second variation where we connected two
separate poses.
The panels above show a static pose joined to a moving pose. In this
approach the dialogue is spoken in the base coverage circle, and as the
two characters walk away side by side dialogue continues. We could
just as easily have the moving pose ﬁrst, ending in the base coverage
circle as a static shot. The earlier variations could also be added; for
example, the current static/moving shot order could begin with Anne
approaching camera, instead of the static shot.

pose to pose
Your actors arrive. You don’t have a storyboard. Set up the scene for
basic coverage. Then set up another staging that you think will work
with the second half of the scene. Now you have two poses, but you
can have more, of course. The actors will connect them. They may be
resistant if it feels awkward, but if you think of emotional moments
when a change in staging supports the story, then set that pose up. The
actors and cinematographer collaborate with the director to work this
out.
The takeaway here is that you learn to think in poses from the camera
view. You can build complex and intricate staging by connecting
poses.

open it up
Try using an approach shot where a character walks to the base
staging area. This can replace the master shot, although you will shoot
one for protection.
During the approach, Anne can already begin the dialogue before she
reaches the man. In the exit shot the man can remain positioned facing
the camera, allowing for a nice reaction shot while Anne departs in
the background. As you look at the panels above, consider variations
using the A, I, L positions.

why stop there?
If we can connect two poses, we can just as easily connect an
unlimted number of poses in a single scene.

working with actors
Most of the book explains staging in an analytical way, which is the
best way to learn in the beginning. However, actors are the life’s blood
of a motion picture and their process is emotional. Even though your
storyboard may be great, if the actor has an alternative approach, it’s
important to listen. Complex choreography requiring the actor to
precisely hit marks is a distraction, and repeated takes are tiring for
the actor. A good director learns how to adapt to the actors and also
how to help them work through the challenges of complex staging
when the storyboard must be followed.
In situations where there is no storyboard, the process of staging
means walking through the action explained in the script. As you walk
through the scene, think in terms of poses (shots). Then you rehearse.
Let the actors do what they think their characters would do. See
anything you like? If so, use it.
Sometimes it’s useful to show the storyboard to the the actors, but
from their point of view, knowing where to hit their marks is one more
thing to think about that distracts from their process. One way to help
is to remain focused on the actor’s performance and allow the
cinematographer or assistant director to adjust the staging. You will
concentrate on the performance. You need to protect the actor from
becoming frustrated with the ﬁlmmaking process. If you provide the
actors a consistent emotional goal for a scene the technical
requirements are far less distracting for them.

25 Parting Shots
The future of storytelling. Where have we been and where are we going.
Big questions, but if we look at the arc of technology beginning in the
ﬁfteenth century with the development of linear perspective, the general
trend has been to replicate reality, or at least our perception of it. This trend
has been accelerating for 500 years, but the digitization of all media has
accelerated the acceleration. Dramatically. One century of improvements in
ﬁlm stock is equal to about 15 years’ development of digital image sensors.
Even more dramatic is the change in the way movies are disseminated. The
theatrical movie experience is competing with increasingly impressive
home theater installations but even the latest 90-inch ﬂat panels are not
cutting edge. Why? Because direct input to the brain via VR glasses will be
available within a few years, with lasers projecting directly on the eye’s
retina, a major advance over glasses placing miniature video displays in
front of each eye. This is all in its infancy but this and other neural-based
approaches to delivering data to the brain are going to dominate the second
half of 21st century media. VR may launch, fail, and launch again within the
next decade, but the goal of direct input to the brain by a future technology
is going to be the holy grail of media companies, and VR is just the
beginning. We are in the age of neuroscience. Give it about 25 years to
become a must-have consumer product.
For those of us steeped in traditional ﬁlm and the almost two-century-old
love affair with silver chemistry, change at the current rate means the end to
cherished values and processes, but in the end a ﬁlmmaker has a story to tell
and the tools are secondary. We at least have the choice to make them
secondary, even though we live in an age of eye candy and short attention
spans, with the emphasis on techno ﬂash. Commercial ﬁlmmaking is still
the blisteringly competitive business for indie ﬁlmmakers that it’s been for
more than 50 years. The alternative is reaching a small audience on YouTube or some other online distributor we haven’t seen yet. This is really all
a ﬁlmmaker needs, but it may not be a living. While this may sound dour,

it’s actually a hugely positive development. Right now, you can take your
iPhone and make a short. You can upload your short in a few days and you
can begin to build an audience. You don’t need investors or distributors.
You don’t have to license your copyright to others. You don’t have to listen
to a half-dozen gatekeepers demanding changes to your work. So what are
you waiting for …?

## Glossary

GLOSSARY
This partial list of terms includes the basic technical vocabulary used in this
book. A more complete collection of ﬁlm terms can be found in How to
Read a Film, by James Monaco; The VES Handbook of Visual Effects:
Industry Standard VFX Practices, by Jeffrey A. Okun and Susan Zwerman;
Focal Encyclopedia of Film and Television Techniques, by Raymond
Spottiswoode; and the current standard comprehensive dictionary of ﬁlm,
The Complete Film Dictionary, 2nd Edition, by Ira Konigsberg.
A
AERIAL or BIRD’S EYE SHOT A shot taken from a very high angle,
most often a wide establishing shot. Drones, with their ability to affordably
acquire high-angle video in remote or difﬁcult locations, have increased the
use of these shots.
AMERICAN SOCIETY OF CINEMATOGRAPHERS (ASC) A
nonproﬁt craft organization of cinematographers with headquarters in
Hollywood, California. This organization publishes both the American
Cinematographer Manual and The American Cinematographer, a monthly
magazine.
ANGLE OF VIEW The size of the ﬁeld covered by a lens measured in
degrees. A wide-angle lens has a wide angle of view while a telephoto lens
has a narrow angle of view. Because a rectangular aperture masks the ﬁlm,
the angle of view for any given lens is described according to the height and
width of the aperture.
ASPECT RATIO The relationship between the height and width of a
motion-picture or television frame expressed numerically. Since the advent
of digital projection standards there is one dominant projection aspect ratio,
16:9; HDTV has varying resolutions, including 2k, 4k, and 5k, with 8k also
being introduced. A wider aspect ratio on the order of CinemaScope is
accomplished by reducing the height of the picture.

B
BEAT BOARDS Simple thumbnail sketches of key moments in a story or
“story beats.” Typically used after a shot list and as preparation for a more
detailed storyboard.
BLOCKING THE SCENE The arrangement and choreographed
movement of actors and major props (e.g., automobiles, horses) for the
camera. Also called staging. Since the camera can also move, blocking the
scene may include the movement of the camera.
BOOM 1. Camera boom. A mechanical arm counterweighted to support a
camera and attached to a dolly. Operated manually or by remote control, it
permits a technician to move the camera over or through spaces that would
otherwise be inaccessible. 2. Microphone boom. A telescoping arm that
supports a microphone. The soundman positions the microphone just
outside the camera frame while recording sound within the scene.
C
CAMERA ANGLE The viewpoint chosen by the director or
cinematographer to photograph a subject.
CAMERA ANGLE PROJECTION A method of turning the architectural
plan and elevation for a set into a perspective drawing for the purpose of
previewing how the ﬁnished set will appear to the camera when using a
particular lens, camera position, and aspect ratio.
CANTED FRAME Sometimes called Chinese or Dutch angles. The
angling of the camera so that the horizontal frame line is not parallel to the
horizon. A slightly canted frame dynamically charges the composition,
while extreme angling has become a conventional way of conveying
disorder and mayhem. The canted frame was used extensively in Carol
Reed’s The Third Man.
CHOKER SHOT Essentially a close-up, most often including the chin to
top of hair with a little room at the top and bottom.
CONTINUITY STYLE The photographic and editorial style that creates
the illusion of a spatial/temporal continuum so that a sequence of shots
appears to present events as they happened. The continuity style is the

predominant style of narrative ﬁlms, sometimes called the Hollywood
classical style or in France, “découpage classique.”
COUNTERING Camera movement that is in the opposite direction of a
moving subject.
COVER SHOT See MASTER SHOT.
CROSS-CUTTING The alternating of shots from two or more scenes to
indicate parallel action.
CUTAWAY A single shot inserted into a sequence that momentarily
interrupts the general ﬂow of action. The inserted shot may introduce a
pertinent detail or a new location related to the action, or a POV shot.
D
DEEP FOCUS A style of cinematography and staging that uses wide-angle
lenses and small lens apertures to emphasize depth by keeping objects in
the extreme foreground and background in focus simultaneously.
DEPTH OF FIELD The amount of space in front of and behind the focus
plane that appears acceptably sharp through a lens.
DETAIL SHOT A tighter, more highly magniﬁed version of the close-up
used to show a fragment of a whole subject or a small object in its entirety.
DIGITAL INTERMEDIATE (D. I.) The process of digitally color
correcting a ﬁnished ﬁlm/video in postproduction performed in a real-time
DI suite, typically with the director present.
DOLLY SHOT A shot made from a moving dolly. Also called a tracking
or traveling shot.
DRONE SHOT A shot made from a quad copter or drone.
E
ESTABLISHING SHOT A shot to introduce a new location in a movie,
typically a wide shot from a high angle, and the ﬁrst shot of the new scene.
However, while many directors establish this way, directors such as Steven
Spielberg may begin a scene in close-up and pull back to a wide shot — an
inherently more provocative approach. See AERIAL SHOT.

F
FLASH CUT An extremely brief shot, as short as one frame, which is
nearly subliminal in effect. Also, a series of short staccato shots that create
a rhythmic effect.
FOCUS IN, OUT A transition effect by which an incoming shot comes
into focus or an outgoing shot goes out of focus.
FOCUS PULL/FOLLOW FOCUS The refocusing of the lens during a
shot to keep a moving subject within the depth of ﬁeld.
FOLLOW SHOT A tracking shot, pan, or zoom move that follows a
moving subject.
FORMS, OPEN AND CLOSED Styles of graphic composition. OPEN
FORMS tend to emphasize looser, less controlled positioning of elements in
the frame as if they were unplanned, extending beyond the boundaries of
the frame as though the camera just discovered them. Open-form
compositions are characteristic of documentary ﬁlms, accounting for their
reputation as being more realistic. CLOSED FORMS tend toward selfcontained compositions carefully arranged to include everything of
importance within the frame.
FRONTALITY A style of ﬁgure placement in composition, popular in
Western art, in which the subject of a painting or drawing faces the general
direction of the viewer.
I
INSERT, INSERT SHOT Usually a close-up showing an important detail
of a scene.
INTERCUT See CROSS-CUTTING.
J
JIB ARM A mechanical arm supported on a dolly and counterweighted to
hold the camera for an increased range of motion, usually vertical.
JUMP CUT A linkage of shots in a scene in which the appearance of real
continuous time has been interrupted by omission. In certain situations,
jump cuts are an accepted convention for compressing time. They can also

be used as a deliberately disruptive device to make the viewer aware of ﬁlm
technique, as in the work of Godard. Recent trends have seen the use of the
staccato jump cut in music videos and commercials for purely rhythmic
effect.
L
LOCKED-OFF CAMERA When the camera is on a tripod or other stable
support, the mechanism that controls the movement of the camera, such as a
ﬂuid or geared head, can be locked in position for a static shot.
LOOSE Refers to the composition of a shot. Loose framing includes a
great deal of space around a subject.
M
MARK As in “hit your mark.” The mark in this case is the place the actor
is supposed to be. Most often the mark is a location in the scene an actor is
going to. It could be a few feet away from the actor’s position or across a
room. The mark is typically identiﬁed with a piece of colored adhesive tape
placed on the ﬂoor, out of camera view but visible to the actor in the scene.
MASTER/MASTER SHOT The viewpoint of a scene in which the
relationships between subjects are clear and the entire dramatic action could
be understood if no other shots were used (as opposed to the wider
establishing shot).
MATCH CUT A transition effect between shots in a scene, or as a
transition. 1. Two shots of the same action joined so that continuous motion
is preserved. 2. Two or more shots of the same subject joined to preserve
continuous placement and motion in each shot to indicate the passage of
time. 3. Two shots of different subjects that share graphic elements or
motion with precisely registered contours. For example, an image of the
moon in a ﬁrst shot lines up exactly with a circular mirror in a second shot.
In Bob Fosse’s All That Jazz, several dancers in a rehearsal hall are seen
from one angle executing the same steps. Match cuts connect the actions as
if they were performed by one dancer in an unbroken movement.
MISE-EN-SCÈNE In French, “putting in the scene.” Originally, the term
described the physical production of a play: the sets, props, and staging of a

scene. Over the years, the term has been adapted to the description of ﬁlmic
space — that is, the manipulation of staging and action within a shot during
ﬁlming as opposed to the manipulation of space afterwards in the editorial
process.
MONTAGE From the French term “to assemble.” In European ﬁlm,
montage is a description of the editorial process. In Soviet ﬁlm, under the
inﬂuence of the early Soviet directors, particularly Sergei Eisenstein,
montage represented the very essence of ﬁlm art. In the United States,
montage has a special meaning, referring to a concentrated narrative device,
usually lasting only a minute, in which a portion of the story is expressed
without dialogue using a series of dissolves to connect short, expressive,
often symbolic shots.
MOVING SHOT A shot in which the camera is moved to follow a moving
subject.
O
OFF CAMERA (OC) Same as offscreen.
OFFSCREEN (OS) Direction in a screenplay when a subject or sound is
outside of the camera frame.
ON CAMERA Term indicating that a player or object can be seen by the
camera.
OTS (Over The Shoulder shot) A composition in which an actor or
character is placed on the left or right side of the camera, but past the
vertical center of the frame with their back to the camera. This leaves room
on the opposite side of the frame for the actor (who is facing the camera).
This staging is very common in dialogue scenes. In addition to over-theshoulder framings, the same idea works over the hip, or over the knee.
Essentially, the OTS uses one actor in a scene as a framing device.
Typically, the OTS is part of a Shot, Reverse Shot pattern.
P
PERSPECTIVE In the graphic arts and ﬁlm, the illusion of depth on a
two-dimensional surface.

PIXEL A physical point in a raster image or more simply, the smallest
controllable element of a digital picture represented on a screen. Pixels are
the little square tiles that you see when you zoom into a digital image.
POINT OF VIEW (POV) SHOT A subjective viewpoint, one that is
understood to represent an individual’s vision.
PRESENCE 1. In audio recording, the ambient sound characteristic of a
room or location. 2. The term used by André Bazin to identify the illusion
of real third-dimensional space that the viewer experiences when watching
a ﬁlm. Bazin regarded this as a further development of Renaissance linear
perspective.
PREVISUALIZATION/PREVIS/PREVIZ The prototyping of a ﬁlm
sequence using digital technology.
PRINCIPAL PHOTOGRAPHY The main photography of a ﬁlm and the
period of time during which it takes place.
PULLBACK SHOT A dolly shot or zoom effect that begins in close-up on
an object and slowly widens to include more of the area surrounding the
object.
R
REACTION SHOT In a dialogue scene the shot of a player listening while
another player’s voice continues on the sound track. Most reaction shots are
close-ups.
RETRACKING The backward movement of the camera on a dolly (on or
off track) along a path that it has already covered in the same shot or in a
previous shot in the same scene.
REVERSE ANGLE A shot that is turned approximately 180 degrees in
relation to the preceding shot.
S
SCRATCH TRACK A temporary voice recording for animation or
previsualization in which inexpensive voice talent or members of the
creative team are substituted for bankable voice talent (movie stars) during
preproduction.

SEQUENCE SHOT A traveling camera shot in which choreographed
action and camera movement replace the need for individual shots to
describe complex action.
SETUP The choice of camera angle, shot size, and staging. It is frequently
described by the number of players in a shot: A two-shot, over-the-shoulder
shot, and close-up are all typical setups.
SHALLOW FOCUS A shallow depth of ﬁeld. Shallow focus is sometimes
used to isolate a subject in focus from the indistinct background and
foreground.
SHOT, REVERSE SHOT or SHOT, COUNTER SHOT An editing and
compositional pattern typical of dialogue sequences in which the camera
frames a subject (the shot) and cuts to a matching (reverse) shot from a new
angle approximately 180 degrees from the ﬁrst. This is a staple of dialogue
sequences or any situation where there are two or more subjects interacting.
The pair of shots is alternated with dialogue and each shot is a mirror image
of the previous shot, but alternating between the actors. However, a Shot,
Reverse Shot pattern is not restricted to dialogue and can also work with
any subjects.
SIGHT LINE An imaginary line that is drawn between a subject and the
object he or she is looking at. When individual CUs of two subjects in
conversation are joined together in a sequence their sight lines must match
in order that they appear to be looking at each other.
SINGLE A shot with only one subject in the frame.
STATIC CAMERA Any shot in which the camera does not move.
SUBJECTIVE CAMERA A camera technique that presents the viewpoint
of a character in the scene. See POINT OF VIEW (POV) SHOT.
SWISH PAN A rapid panning movement that blurs the frame. It is often
used as a transition. Also called a FLASH PAN.
T
TAKE One version of a shot. The director has the cast and crew repeat the
action of the shot in successive takes until he or she is satisﬁed it is correct.

TIGHT Describes the size of a shot relative to its subject. A tight frame
encloses a subject with very little space surrounding it.
TRACKING SHOT A shot taken from a camera mounted on a dolly or
some other moving vehicle.
TRAVELING SHOT Any shot in which the camera moves through space.
A crane shot and a tracking shot are both traveling shots. A pan shot,
however, is not a traveling shot since the camera remains at one location
pivoting a stationary point.
TWO-SHOT A shot that frames two people.
V
VOICE-OVER (VO) The voice of an unseen narrator. Voice-over may also
be used to indicate the thoughts of a character in a scene when the character
is not speaking.
W
WIPE A transition between shots.
WRAP The ofﬁcial end of a day’s shooting.
Z
ZIP PAN See SWISH PAN.
ZOOM The movement of a zoom lens or the lens itself.

ONLINE RESOURCES
Many principles of ﬁlmmaking and design are the same in both the analog
and digital realms. This is not true of computer software, where the
majority of ﬁlm design and production take place. This includes shooting
on location if you are using a digital camera that is a species of computer
too. Things change yearly/monthly, so http://shotbyshotbook.com will
provide more up-to-date information on digital resources available online.
There’s a lot of it, but the quality varies.
Here are some of the best and hopefully stable sources of information and
learning for the nitty-gritty of digital production and software.

Lynda.com
In-depth training in popular software such as Adobe: After Effects,
Photoshop, Premiere, Cinema 4D, Maya, and dozens of other mediarelated software applications.
Redgiant.com
This is a visual effects software site with great tutorials. They have
positioned themselves to help indie ﬁlmmakers. In addition to VFX
products such as Trapcode, they have many tools for color correction of
indie movies. The folks that run the company are ﬁlmmakers.
Pluralsight.com (formerly Digital Tutors)
Visual effects and game production software training at a high level for
software such as Maya, Cinema 4D, Houdini, ZBrush, Blender,
Photoshop, and many more.
Fxphd.com
Technical training in several key VFX software applications as well as
art/tech theory for digital production.
Cinemetrics.lv
A fascinating scientiﬁc/statistical approach to ﬁlm study by measuring
the length of shots, the number of shots, and other quantitative
measurements over the history of ﬁlm. An academic site by ﬁlm scholar
Barry Salt.

## About the Author

About the Author
Steven D. Katz is an award-winning writer, director, and producer. His
eclectic work includes light shows for the Grateful Dead, shorts for
Saturday Night Live, the ﬁrst major 3D animated movie in China, the ﬁrst
full digital previsualization for a motion picture (Clear and Present
Danger), months spent at the Neverland Ranch as Michael Jackson’s ﬁlm
teacher, and a couple of decades writing about ﬁlm for cinema and art
publications. He lives by the same advice he teaches: Keep making art! For
more information, visit shotbyshotbook.com.

PRODUCER TO PRODUCER
A STEP-BY-STEP GUIDE TO LOW BUDGET INDEPENDENT
FILM PRODUCING
MAUREEN A. RYAN
Everything you need to know – all in one place – to produce your
independent ﬁlm or documentary, from last year’s Academy Awardwinning co-producer of Man on Wire.
All the steps are clearly stated so that you can produce your low
budget independent ﬁlm. Save time and money by following the
advice and information in Producer to Producer to make the most
successful ﬁlm possible.
This book is a deﬁnitive step-by-step guide for low budget
independent ﬁlm producing. Filled with practical advice awardwinning producer Maureen Ryan has culled from her 20-plus years of
production experience, it takes you through the entire process of

bringing a ﬁlm into the world. From development and script
breakdown, to budgeting and preproduction, to production and post,
and to sales and distribution, Producer to Producer gives you all the
information you need in easy-to-follow, chronological order.

VISUAL STORYTELLING
HOW TO SPEAK TO YOUR AUDIENCE WITHOUT SAYING A
WORD
MORGAN SANDLER
If you have ever wondered how professional ﬁlms create such
dynamic images, then this book is for you! Visual Storytelling will
help you craft masterful images without a Hollywood budget. The
book covers everything from basic story structure to composition,
lighting and camera work. Visual Storytelling is a standalone course in
directing and cinematography that gives the readers all the tools they
need to craft a visually compelling ﬁlm.
Visual Storytelling covers all major components of creating powerful
images including lighting, camera functions, composition and
storytelling. However, the main focus of the book is not just creating
compelling visuals, but more importantly creating images that inform
and move the audience. Images carry emotional weight and Visual
Storytelling teaches readers how to harness these emotions to
maximize the emotion of the story, while minimizing the amount of
dialogue necessary.

What makes Visual Storytelling unique is that it not only covers the
theoretical concepts of ﬁlmmaking but also the technical elements
necessary to achieve the emotional outcome. This combination of
theory and practice helps to create well informed and skilled
ﬁlmmakers.
■ Covers numerous areas of ﬁlmmaking, creating well informed and
skilled ﬁlmmakers.
■ Written in a very conversational and approachable style making it
easy for new ﬁlmmakers to digest.
■ Sandler is a professional ﬁlmmaker as well as a university ﬁlm
professor.
■ The book can be used as a textbook in schools to accompany
beginner and intermediate ﬁlm courses or as a standalone text.
“Sandler, a cinematographer, covers every visual tool in detail, from
the direction and intensity of lighting, to the lenses and focus used. A
brilliant read for those aspiring to be in his position.”
—Kathryn Butt, Raindance
MORGAN SANDLER is a professor of Digital Film Production at the
University of La Verne, in Southern California. Previously, he helped

University, Los Angeles, where he also served as an adjunct professor.

to shape many courses and design a large portion of the curriculum.

CINEMATIC STORYTELLING
THE 100 MOST POWERFUL FILM CONVENTIONS EVERY
FILMMAKER MUST KNOW
JENNIFER VAN SIJLL
BEST SELLER
How do directors use screen direction to suggest conﬂict? How do
screenwriters exploit ﬁlm space to show change? How does editing
style determine emotional response?
Many ﬁrst-time writers and directors do not ask these questions. They
forego the huge creative resource of the ﬁlm medium, defaulting to
dialog to tell their screen story. Yet most movies are carried by sound
and picture. The industry’s most successful writers and directors have
mastered the cinematic conventions speciﬁc to the medium. They
have harnessed non-dialog techniques to create some of the most
cinematic moments in movie history.
This book is intended to help writers and directors more fully exploit
the medium’s inherent storytelling devices. It contains 100 non-dialog
techniques that have been used by the industry’s top writers and
directors. From Metropolis and Citizen Kane to Dead Man and Kill

Bill, the book illustrates — through 500 frame grabs and 75 script
excerpts — how the inherent storytelling devices speciﬁc to ﬁlm were
exploited.
You will learn:
■ How non-dialog ﬁlm techniques can advance story.
■ How master screenwriters exploit cinematic conventions to
create powerful scenarios.
“Cinematic Storytelling scores a direct hit in terms of concise
information and perfectly chosen visuals, and it also searches out…
and ﬁnds… an emotional core that many books of this nature either
miss or are afraid of.”
—Kirsten Sheridan, Director, Disco Pigs; Co-writer, In America

to the ﬁeld. Jennifer van Sijll takes her readers in a wholly new
direction, integrating aspects of screenwriting with all the ﬁlm crafts
in a way I’ve never before seen. It is essential reading not only for
screenwriters but also for ﬁlmmakers of every stripe.”
—Prof. Richard Walter, UCLA Screenwriting Chairman
JENNIFER VAN SIJLL has taught ﬁlm production, ﬁlm history, and

{ THE MYTH OF MWP }
In a dark time, a light bringer came along, leading the curious and the
frustrated to clarity and empowerment. It took the well-guarded secrets out
of the hands of the few and made them available to all. It spread a spirit of
openness and creative freedom, and built a storehouse of knowledge
dedicated to the betterment of the arts.
The essence of the Michael Wiese Productions (MWP) is empowering
people who have the burning desire to express themselves creatively. We
help them realize their dreams by putting the tools in their hands. We
demystify the sometimes secretive worlds of screenwriting, directing,
acting, producing, ﬁlm ﬁnancing, and other media crafts.
By doing so, we hope to bring forth a realization of ‘conscious media’
which we deﬁne as being positively charged, emphasizing hope and
afﬁrming positive values like trust, cooperation, self-empowerment,
freedom, and love. Grounded in the deep roots of myth, it aims to be
healing both for those who make the art and those who encounter it. It
hopes to be transformative for people, opening doors to new possibilities
and pulling back veils to reveal hidden worlds.
MWP has built a storehouse of knowledge unequaled in the world, for no
other publisher has so many titles on the media arts. Please visit
www.mwp.com where you will ﬁnd many free resources and a 25%
discount on our books. Sign up and become part of the wider creative
community!
Onward and upward,

Michael Wiese
Publisher/Filmmaker
